**B**ecause God willed that
every kind of joy
humans should naturally
possess within themselves,
so <term>that</term><note>The original uses the shorthand "q" for "que."</note>

ships when they be-

many ways through which this
joy might be obtained com-
pletely. ¶ Wherefore, for this reason,
they found and devised many
kinds of games and <term>pastimes</term><note>original: "trebeios." In medieval Spanish, this term referred to both the games themselves and the physical pieces or equipment used to play them.</note> with which
to enjoy themselves. ¶ Some are played on
horseback, such as <term>jousting with reeds</term><note>original: "bohordar." A mock-combat sport where riders threw light wooden reeds at one another to practice maneuvers without the lethality of a real tournament.</note>
and spear-throwing and taking up the shield and casting and
shooting with a crossbow or a bow; or other
games of whatever kind they
may be that can be performed on
horseback. ¶ And although these
are taken up for the use and benefit of the
art of war, because they are not war itself,
they call them games. ¶ The others
that are done on foot are such as

fencing, wrestling, running, jumping, throwing a
stone or a dart, hitting a ball, and other games
of many natures in which humans use
their limbs so that they may become
stronger and receive joy through it.

<term>dice</term><note>original: "dados."</note>, and other <term>pastimes</term> of many kinds.

games are very good, each in the

yet because these games played while seated
are done together, they can be played as well by night

who do not ride horses and stay indoors, are to

those who face harsh weather, because
they cannot ride nor go hunting

<meta>This page continues the prologue of the "Book of Games" (Libro de los Juegos), commissioned by King Alfonso X of Castile. The previous page established that games are a gift for humans to find joy and relief from the hardships of life. This page moves from the general physical games (jousting, running) to the sedentary games of the mind, introducing the royal commission and the philosophical debate regarding skill versus luck.</meta>

->### BOOK OF THE<-

in their houses and to seek some types of games with which they might have pleasure and find solace, and not be idle.

<image-desc>A large miniature spanning the width of both text columns. It depicts three figures seated at writing desks within a triple-arched architectural frame. The arches are golden yellow with patterned details and rest on slender columns. The background behind the figures is a solid blue decorated with white filigree. On the left, a youth in a pink robe writes in a book. In the center, a man in an ochre-colored tunic is likewise engaged in writing. On the right, an older scholar or monk in a dark grey hooded habit writes at his desk. The scene symbolizes the scholarly labor of composing and transcribing the manuscript.</image-desc>

Among us, **Don Alfonso**, by the grace of God, King of **Castile**, of **Toledo**, of **León**, of **Galicia**, of **Seville**, of **Córdoba**, of **Murcia**, of **Jaén**, and of the **Algarve** <note>Alfonso X (1221–1284), known as "The Wise" (El Sabio), was a polymath king who sponsored the translation of scientific, legal, and literary works.</note>: we commanded this book to be made in which we speak of the manner of those games that are most elegant, such as <term>chess</term><note>original: "açedrex," from the Arabic "al-shatranj."</note>, dice, and <term>tables</term><note>original: "tablas." A family of board games including the ancestors of backgammon.</note>. And although these games are divided into many kinds: because chess is the most noble and requires the greatest <term>mastery</term><note>original: "maestria." Refers to the intellectual skill and strategy required for chess compared to games of pure chance.</note> than the others, we speak of it first. But before we say this, we wish to show some reasons according to what the ancient sages said as to why these three kinds of games were found: namely chess, dice, and tables. For regarding this, they gave many reasons, each one telling how these games were discovered. But those [reasons] that are most certain and

<column-break/>

truest are these:
According to what is told in the ancient histories, in **Greater India** there was a king who loved the sages much and kept them always with him; and he often made them reason about the deeds that arise from things. And of these, there were three who held distinct arguments. One said that <term>reason</term><note>original: "seso." This term encompasses intellect, common sense, and strategic skill.</note> was worth more than <term>luck</term><note>original: "uentura." This refers to fortune, fate, or the random chance of the dice.</note>. For he who lives by reason does things in an orderly fashion; and even if he should lose, he was not at fault, since he did what was fitting. The other said that luck was worth more than reason; for if luck determined one should lose or win, by no amount of reason he had could he escape it. The third said that it was better if one could proceed by taking from one and from the other: for this was true living. For in reason, the better it was, the greater help it provided, so that it could be performed completely. And likewise in luck, the greater it was, the greater the danger, because it is not a certain thing. But the right <term>wisdom</term><note>original: "cordura." A balanced judgment or prudence that combines intellectual skill with an understanding of external circumstances.</note> was: to take...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical introduction to the "Book of Games." The previous page introduced King Alfonso X and established chess, dice, and tables as the primary subjects. This text describes a legendary debate between three wise men regarding whether life (and games) is governed by skill, luck, or a combination of both.</meta>

...from <term>intellect</term><note>original: "sesso." In this medieval context, this refers to human reason, logic, and strategic skill.</note> that which a man understood would be most to his benefit. And from <term>fortune</term><note>original: "uentura." Referring to luck, chance, or fate.</note>, a man should guard himself against harm as much as he could, and help himself with it in whatever would be to his advantage.

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature framed by Gothic architectural elements, including three arches supported by columns and crenellated towers at the corners. And after they had spoken their very firm arguments: the King commanded them to bring their books, each according to his reasoning. And when the time arrived: each one came before the King with his example. ¶ And he who held the argument for **intellect**: brought **chess** with its games, showing that he who had the greatest intellect and was most alert could defeat the other. ¶ And the second, who held the argument for **fortune**: brought the **dice**, showing that intellect was worth nothing without luck. As it appeared by the roll of the dice, bringing a man benefit or harm through it. ¶ The third, who said it was better to take from the one and from the other: brought the game board with

<column-break/>

its <term>pieces</term><note>original: "tablas." These are the circular counters used in "tables," the medieval precursor to backgammon.</note> counted and placed in their spaces in an orderly manner, and with the dice: so that they might move them to play, as is shown in this book which speaks specifically of this. In which it is made to be understood that through the playing of them: he who knew how to play them well—even if the luck of the dice were against him—could, through his **wisdom**, play with the pieces in such a way that he would avoid the harm that might come to him through the chance of the dice.

<meta>This page continues the introduction to King Alfonso X’s "Book of Games." Having previously introduced the three wise men who debated the merits of intellect, fortune, and a combination of both, this text now focuses on why chess was chosen as the superior game and outlines the contents of the treatise.</meta>

<image-desc>A large illumination at the top of the page depicts an architectural setting with three pointed arches and decorative towers. Beneath the leftmost arch, a bearded king (presumably Alfonso X of Castile) sits on an ornate throne, wearing a blue robe and a golden crown. He gestures with his right hand toward three men seated on the ground under the adjacent arches. The first man holds a square chessboard; the second holds a rectangular board with dice markings; the third holds a long tables (backgammon) board. The reason why **Chess**<note>original: "Acedrex." This medieval Spanish term is derived from the Arabic "al-shatranj."</note> is a more reasoned game than the others, including **Tables**<note>original: "tablas." This refers to a family of board games played with pieces and dice, the most famous survivor being Backgammon.</note>: this book speaks first of the manner in which the board must be made; how many squares it has; and what the games are; and how many; and what the name of each piece is; and in which squares they must be placed; and how they move while playing with them; and how they capture one another; and what advantages some have over the others. And how the players must be prepared to know how to play in such a way that they win and are not defeated; and how to give <term>check</term><note>original: "rraque." Derived from the Persian word for "King."</note> to the king, which is the greatest obligation of all others, for it is a way of confronting the lord with right; and how they give him <term>mate</term><note>original: "mate." From "shāh māt," meaning "the king is dead" or "the king is helpless."</note> which is a form of great dishonor, as if they had defeated or killed him. ¶ And there are many other types of games. But all were made in the likeness of things that 

<column-break/>

occurred according to the times that were, or are, or could be, showing how kings in the time of wars—in which armies are formed—go following their enemies, striving to defeat them, capturing them and killing them or driving them from the land. ¶ And likewise, how in the time of peace they must show their treasures and their riches and the noble and strange things they possess. And according to this, they made games: some of fourteen squares, others of ten, others of eight, others of seven, others of four, and so they went descending even to a single square that they divided into eight parts. ¶ And all this they did through great symbolism according to the <term>ancient knowledge</term><note>original: "saberes antigos." This refers to the classical and Hermetic traditions that Alfonso X sought to preserve and translate.</note> that the <term>wise men</term><note>original: "sabios."</note> used. ¶ But among all the other games, they chose as the best and most balanced that of the **eight squares**<note>The 8x8 grid of the standard chessboard.</note>, because it is not as slow as the game of ten or more, nor is it as hurried as those of six or seven and below. ¶ And for this reason, men commonly use it.

<summary>This page explains why chess is the most "reasoned" game, comparing its movements to medieval warfare and its structure to ancient philosophical proportions. It also establishes the 8x8 board as the ideal balance between a game that is too slow or too fast.</summary>
<keywords>Chess, Acedrex, Tables, Alfonso X, military strategy, symbolism, board games, checkmate, medieval philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the game of chess, specifically describing its physical components and the symbolic meaning of its pieces as a representation of an army. It follows the previous page's discussion on why chess was chosen as the most intellectual game.</meta>

# CHESS <note>original: "ACE DREX." A medieval Spanish spelling derived from the Arabic "al-shatranj."</note>

...in all armies, more than any other games. And the shape of the board is that it must be square, and it must have eight rows, and in each row eight squares, which make sixty-four squares in all. And half of the squares must be of one color, and the other half of another, just as with the **pieces** <note>original: "trebejos," a term used for game pieces or tools.</note>.

---

<image-desc>A tripartite gothic arcade with decorative gold and blue arches. Under the left arch, a man in a light robe sits at a table examining a wooden board. The central arch contains a large, vertical, checkered board with red and gold squares. Under the right arch, a craftsman in an orange tunic sits at a bench carving a chess piece, with several finished pieces displayed on a shelf above him.</image-desc>

## Law <note>A large decorative initial L serves both for the word "Ley" (Law/Rule) and the subsequent "Los" (The).</note> regarding how many colors all the chess pieces should be.
<gloss>Regarding how they should be set on the board.</gloss>
The pieces must be of one type. And the sixteen of one color must be set in the first two rows of the board. And another sixteen of the other color must be set on the other side of the board in that same manner, directly across from the others. And of these sixteen pieces, eight are smaller, for they were made in the likeness of the **common people** who go in the army. And the other pieces, which are larger: one is in the likeness of the **King**, who is the lord of the army, and he must stand in one of the middle squares. And beside him, in the other middle square, is another piece which is in the likeness of the **Standard-bearer** <note>original: "alfferez."</note> who holds the banner

<column-break/>

of the King’s signals; and some men who do not know the name call it the <term>alferza</term> <note>original: "alfferza." Derived from the Arabic "al-firzan" (vizier/counselor). This piece eventually became the modern Queen.</note>. Neither of these two pieces is like any other among the sixteen pieces. And in the other two squares beside these stand two other pieces that look alike, and they are called <term>alfils</term> <note>original: "alffiles."</note> in **Arabic** <note>original: "algarabia."</note>, which means the same in our language as **elephants**, which Kings used to take into battles; each took at least two so that if one died, the other would remain for him. And in the other two squares beside these are two other pieces that look alike and are commonly called **horses**, but their proper names are **knights**, who are placed as a guard by the King’s command to organize the **ranks** <note>original: "azes," referring to military formations or lines of battle.</note> of the army. And in the other two squares at the end
<meta>catchword: bo (from "cabo")</meta>

<summary>This page describes the physical layout of a chessboard (64 squares in an 8x8 grid) and begins explaining the symbolic military hierarchy represented by the pieces, including the King, Standard-bearer (Queen), Elephants (Bishops), and Knights.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the game of chess, transitioning from the physical layout of the board to the symbolic roles and specific movements of each piece, equating them to ranks and maneuvers in a medieval army.</meta>

# BOOK OF [CHESS]

Among the pieces, there are others that resemble one another. They are named by those who set them on the boards, being made in the likeness of the ranks of knights <note>In this context, the text refers to the "aces" or formations of an army.</note>.

In the first row are the major pieces we mentioned. ¶ And in the second, the **pawns**. ¶ And although these pieces are nine in terms of the squares they occupy, they are no more than six types according to how they are categorized. ¶ The **bishops**, the **knights**, and the **rooks**, which are six pieces [three pairs], are joined with the **king**, the **queen** <note>original: "alferza." In medieval chess, this piece represented a vizier or general and was much weaker than the modern queen.</note>, and the **pawns** who travel a path of their own; thus they make six types. ¶ And they placed them doubled in this way so that if any of those [pairs] are lost, another of the same nature remains to guard and checkmate the [opposing] king, or to protect their own. ¶ Likewise, they ruled regarding the **queen** that if she were lost, and any of the **pawns** were able to reach the very first square of the king on the opposing side—from where the major pieces move—that pawn must be made a "queen" <note>original: "alferzada." This refers to the rule of pawn promotion.</note> so they could be reinforced just as the first one was, and move in that same manner.

This is because they rise from the status of the lesser people to that of the greater.

Regarding the **king**, they ruled that he could not be captured, but that they could give him "check" <note>original: "xaque."</note> so that they could make him leave that place where he resided, as one who is dishonored. ¶ And if they cornered him in such a way that he had no square to go to, they gave it the name "checkmate" <note>original: "xamat." Derived from the Persian "Shah Mat," meaning "the King is dead."</note>, which is as much as to say "dead." And they did this to bring the game to an end. For it would be greatly prolonged if all the pieces had to be captured until both kings were left alone, or only one of them. <insert>Chapter on the movement of the chess pieces.</insert>

The movement of the black pieces was also established for this reason which we shall tell. And just as the king does not have to wander through battles more than one step at a time—always gaining ground from the enemies and striving to defeat them—likewise the king of the pieces must not move more than one square straight ahead, or diagonally, as one who watches all

<column-break/>

parts around himself, turning his mind toward what he must do.

The **queen** <term>alferza|The medieval predecessor to the Queen, representing a military advisor or vizier.</term> moves one square diagonally; and this is to guard the king and not depart from him, and to shield him from checks and mates when they are given; so that by moving forward, she helps to win any well-played game. ¶ But, the first time she moves, she may leap to the third square, either straight or diagonally, even if another piece is in between. And this is in the manner of a good knight <note>Here used in the sense of a noble warrior or champion.</note> who rushes forward into great deeds and battles and brings aid to every part where they have need of him. ¶ And in this movement, joining with his pawns and defeating those [enemies]: there, like faithful subjects, they should strive not to be separated and stay together to do their best; and in this, she protects them while some are before her, and by standing before the others. ¶ And understand that when the queen is thus joined with the pawns, it is called "queen-guarded" <note>original: "alfersada."</note>.

The **bishops** <term>alfiles|From the Arabic "al-fil," meaning "the elephant."</term> leap to the third square diagonally, in the likeness of the **elephants** that kings used to bring [to war]. No one dared to stand before them; those who were in them [the towers on the elephants' backs] struck diagonally to hit the enemy ranks so that they could not protect themselves.

The **knights** leap to the third square, counting two squares straight and then the third diagonally to whichever side they wish. ¶ This is in the likeness of the good knights who lead the ranks, moving their horses to the right and to the left to guard their own and defeat the enemies.

The **rooks** play in a straight line as far as they can go before them, either to the right or to the left. This is in the likeness of the ranks of soldiers who all move in one way to conquer in a straight line or toward whichever part they believe will be best, so they may more quickly defeat those with whom they fight.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic and strategic logic behind the movement of chess pieces, comparing them to medieval military ranks like elephants, knights, and kings. It also details the rules for "xaque" (check), "xamat" (checkmate), and pawn promotion into an "alferza."</summary>
<keywords>chess pieces, alferza, alfil, roque, xamat, pawn promotion, medieval military symbolism, game rules, King Alfonso X</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and symbolic description of the game of chess. While the previous page introduced the pieces as representations of a medieval social and military hierarchy, this page details their specific movements, capturing rules, and strategic advantages, maintaining the metaphor of a battlefield.</meta>

->**CHESS**<-

**Pawns** move no more than one square straight ahead, just like the foot soldiers of an army; they cannot move more than one because they go on foot and carry their weapons and other necessary things on their backs. And although some players allow **pawns** to move to the third square on their first move <note>This refers to an early variation of the double-step initial move for pawns.</note>, this is a forced move that they cannot do if they are "leaping" <note>The text suggests that if the move is used to jump over another piece or in a specific hidden maneuver, it is invalid.</note>. This is done in the likeness of how common people are sometimes granted certain advantages to help them advance.

### Chapter on how the pieces capture in the game of chess.

The **king** captures in all the squares we have said he can move to, taking any piece of the opposing side that he finds there, unless there is another piece from the opposing side that protects that piece. And this same rule applies to the other major pieces, such as the **bishops**, the **knights**, and the **rooks**. However, the **queen** <note>original: "alfferza."</note> cannot capture on her first move if she makes a leap to another square; but after she has made that leap, she may capture on her second move, diagonally, according to her normal movement.

**Pawns** likewise, although they move straight forward on their first move, cannot capture in that direction; instead, they capture diagonally when moving forward one square. And this is in the likeness of foot soldiers who cannot strike one another when standing directly face to face and on guard; instead, a soldier strikes the one who is to his side (diagonally) and is not guarding against him.

### Chapter on the advantages of the pieces in chess.

The advantages that some pieces have over others are significant. For the **king** is protected <note>original: "acotado."</note> in such a way that he can capture everyone, but no one can capture him. This is in the likeness of a king who can execute justice upon all those in his realm, but for this reason, no one may lay a hand on him to seize him, nor strike him, nor kill him, even though he might lose

<column-break/>

or be killed in battle. However, they can put him in "predicaments" <note>original: "iugueras."</note> in three ways: by forcing him to leave the square where he is, by blocking the square he wishes to enter, or by preventing him from capturing what he desires.

The **queen** <note>original: "alfferza."</note> has another type of advantage: because she guards the **king** more closely than the other pieces, she is better than the **bishops** because she has more squares in which she can move and capture than they do. Furthermore, she guards and captures both forward and backward, which the **pawns** cannot do, even if they have been promoted <note>original: "ffagan alfferzada."</note> as mentioned before.

The **bishops** <note>original: "alffiles."</note> have an advantage over the **pawns** because they capture from a greater distance and can also perform a "bishop-defense" <note>original: "alffilada."</note> in this manner: when the **bishop** is on the board, if a **pawn** of his own side is one square away diagonally according to the bishop's movement, the **pawn** guards the **bishop**. If another **pawn** is guarding that first one in the next square where the **bishop** can go, then the **bishop** guards that [second] pawn. In this way, all three guard one another, and this is called an *alffilada*.

The **knight** <note>original: "cauallo."</note> has a greater advantage than all other pieces in chess, except for the **king**. For he who knows how to play the **knight** well and move it from one part of the board to the other will capture as many pieces as there are in all the squares of the board, provided they are three squares away <note>The text describes the "L" shape movement as being three squares away from the starting point.</note> including the square he moves from, where he must never capture.

The **rook** <note>original: "roque."</note> has the greatest advantage of all the other pieces in chess because it can go in one move from one end of the board to the other in a straight line in whichever direction it wishes, provided there is no piece of its own side in the way to block it, or an opponent's piece which it may capture by ending its move on that square.

### Chapter on how the king and all the other chess pieces can move and capture, some in all squares of the board and others in only some.

The **king** can move and capture in all the squares of the board in 57 moves <note>This likely refers to the minimum number of moves a king requires to visit every square on the board or a specific mathematical puzzle common in medieval treatises.</note>.

<summary>This page details the movement and capturing rules for chess pieces, explaining the strategic "advantages" of each while maintaining metaphors of medieval law and warfare.</summary>
<keywords>pawns, king, queen, alferza, bishops, knights, rooks, capturing rules, alffilada, movement advantages</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of piece movements from the previous page and introduces the "Great Chess" variation involving dice. It also transitions from the rules of play to the physical craftsmanship and symbolism of the chess pieces themselves.</meta>

# BOOK

...and return to its starting square.

The **queen** <note>original: "alfferza." In medieval chess, the queen was a much weaker piece called the *alferza* (vizier), moving only one square diagonally.</note> can move into thirty-three squares, which are all the squares on the board that it can reach, and return to its starting square; but we do not count how many times it happens to enter a square twice by necessity.

The **bishop** <note>original: "alffil." Derived from the Arabic *al-fil* (the elephant).</note> can move and capture in six squares of the board plus its own, and no more. <note>In this era, the bishop leaped exactly two squares diagonally, skipping the intermediate square.</note>

The **pawn** can be made a **queen** in seven moves, moving square by square, and once it has been promoted, it can return to its starting square in as many moves as the other **queen**—even as many squares as the board allows it to travel. And even if it enters a square other times, we cannot count it as more than one square.

The movement of the **rook** cannot be counted because it moves both far and near across the whole board, but it always moves straight in all directions, according to its movement.

It is necessary for those who wish to play **chess** <term>açedrez: from the Arabic "al-shatranj," the medieval precursor to modern chess</term> well to know all these movements. For without knowing this, they will not be able to understand the "divided games" <note>original: "iuegos departidos." These are chess problems or puzzles, a popular form of gambling and study in the Middle Ages.</note> that men must play. Because of the boredom people feel from the length of the "greater game" <note>The full, standard game of chess.</note> when it is played completely, for that same reason other variations were made in **chess** so that the game would be more lively.

They assigned the six, which is the highest roll of the die, to the **king**, who is the most honored piece on the board. And the five to the **queen**. And the four to the **rook**. And the three to the **knight**. And the two to the **bishop**. And the single point which they call the "ace" to the **pawn**.

And because **chess** games are divided into many styles—even though they create specific problems within them—in some variations all the **kings** are captured, and in others, they are not. We wish to speak here first of the game that is played with all the pieces complete, to show how the board is made and the positions of the **kings**; but those that are made best and most completely must be fashioned in this manner:

<column-break/>

# OF THE

The **king** should be sitting in his chair with his crown on his head and his sword in his hand, just as if he were judging or commanding his justice. His **queen** <note>original: "alfferza."</note> should be made in the likeness of the king's high standard-bearer <note>Alfonso connects the piece's name to the military rank of *alférez*.</note> who carries the royal banner when they go into battle.

The **bishops** are to be made in the likeness of elephants with castles on top of them full of armed men, as if they were going to fight.

The **knights** are to be made in the likeness of armed knights, like those who are placed by the king’s command to lead the ranks <term>azes: military battle lines or squadrons</term>.

The **rooks** should be made like the ranks of armed knights who stand in a dense formation, supporting one another.

The **pawns** are to be made in the likeness of the common people, who are armed and prepared when they wish to fight. Furthermore, because in all the lands where they play **chess**, they have a great desire to make such pieces as these, men sought a way to make them more easily and with less cost, yet so that they still resembled somewhat those we have described. And the shape of them that is most used in all lands, and especially in **Spain**, is the one that is painted here.

<vocab>chess, queen, bishop, rook, pawn, king, board, Spain, pieces, ranks</vocab>

# CHESS

Now that we have finished explaining the "greater game" of chess and how it is played in full, we wish to speak of the **divided games** <term>uuegos repartidos: chess problems or puzzles where a specific scenario is set up on the board</term> that men have compiled. In these, there are things that are new and strange to hear of, and for this reason, they are highly prized because they are played more quickly. For these are "counted" games, known by how many turns they will take to finish after play begins. However, we will speak first of the larger divided games that are played with all the **chess pieces** <term>trebejos: the individual pieces or "tools" of the game</term>, so that none are missing; and afterward, we shall speak of how they decrease until there are as few as can be. And we wish to speak next of the game called "Forced." And this is because, even though it is played by count, there must be a "force" <note>This refers to the rule of "forced capture," where if a capture is possible, the player MUST take the piece, even if it is a bad move.</note>, for a man acts against his will, losing a better piece for a poorer one; he is brought to do this, whether he wishes to or not, in a

<column-break/>

square that the other must take by force, according to the movement of the piece placed there. And this game is set up exactly like the first <note>Standard 8x8 chess.</note>; the pieces move in the same way and capture one another, except that the "force" is added. And therefore, those who play it must be skillful, so that they do not place their better pieces in a spot where they must give them up for the lesser and more lowly ones. For in this, all the wisdom of this game will appear: in the **division** <note>repartimiento: here referring to the specific layout or strategic arrangement of the problem.</note>. And because of this force we mentioned, it is called the **Forced Game**. Furthermore, because some say that maidens first discovered it in the lands "across the sea" <note>original: "ultra mar." In the 13th century, this usually referred to the Crusader states in the Levant or the Middle East.</note>, it is called the **Game of Maidens**.

<image-desc>A Gothic-style illuminated miniature spanning both columns below the text. The illustration is framed by five architectural arches with blue backgrounds, supported by thin columns and topped with a crenelated roofline. In the center, a large chessboard (8x8 grid with red and white squares) sits on a low platform. Two primary players are seated flanking the board: on the left, a woman in a pink gown and white wimple points toward the board, accompanied by two ladies-in-waiting (one in blue, one in a light tunic); on the right, a woman in a green gown and white wimple reaches to move a piece, accompanied by two other women (one in pink, one in dark red). The scene depicts the "juego de donzellas" (game of maidens) mentioned in the text.</image-desc>

# BOOK OF [CHESS]

This is another **divided game** <term>iuego departido: a chess problem or puzzle with a specific setup</term> in which there are thirty **chess pieces** <term>axedreces: the individual pieces on the board</term> that are to be set up <term>entablados: arranged in a starting position</term> exactly as they appear in the illustration of the setup <term>entablamiento: the diagram showing where pieces are placed</term>, and it is played in this way:

The white pieces play first, either by the roll of the dice or by choice <note>Original: "a los puntos o a mano," referring to different ways to start a game, either using dice points or simply by hand/agreement.</note>. And this must be done in sixteen turns, where **check** <term>xaque: a move that threatens the King</term> is called against the black king <note>Original: "rey prieto," literally the "dark king."</note> every single time using one of the **white knights** <term>cauallos: literally "horses," representing the knight piece</term>. The goal is to make him return to the very square from which he first started when the board was set up.

¶ The **first move** is to give check with the white knight that is in the third square of the black knight; and he [the King] will enter the fourth square of the black rook, or the fourth square of the black bishop. If the black king enters his Rook's square, it is **mate** <term>mate: checkmate, the end of the game</term> on the first move by giving him check with the other white knight in the third square of the black knight. Because of this, it is better for him to enter his bishop’s square.

¶ The **second move** is to give him check with the other white knight in the third square of the black knight; the black king will enter his own square.

¶ The **third move** is to give check with the white knight in the second square of the black knight; the black king will enter the second square of his **queen** <term>alfferza: the "General" or "Vizier," a piece that moved only one square diagonally in medieval chess</term>.

¶ The **fourth move** is to give check with the white knight in the black bishop's <term>alffil: the medieval bishop, which jumped exactly two squares diagonally</term> square; the black king will enter the second square of his bishop.

¶ The **fifth move** is to give check with the white knight in the black king’s square. If the black king enters his knight’s square, it is mate on the first move by giving check with the white knight in the second square of the black queen. Therefore, it is better that he enters the third square of his knight.

¶ The **sixth move** is to give him check with the white knight in the second square of the black queen; the black king will enter the fourth square of his knight.

¶ The **seventh move** is to give check with the white knight in the second square of the black bishop; the black king enters the fourth square of the **white bishop**. For if he entered the fourth square of the white rook, it would be mate on the first move by giving him check with the white knight in

<column-break/>

the fourth square of the black knight.

¶ The **eighth move** is to give check with the white knight in the third square of the black knight; the black king will enter the third square of the white bishop.

¶ The **ninth move** is to give check with the white knight in the fourth square of the black knight, and the black king will enter the second square of the white queen.

¶ The **tenth move** is to give check with the white knight in the fourth square of the white bishop, and the black king will enter the second square of the white king.

¶ The **eleventh move** is to give check with the white knight in the third square of the white bishop; the black king will enter the third square of the other white bishop.

¶ The **twelfth move** is to give check with the white knight in the second square of the white queen, and the black king will enter the third square of the white knight.

¶ The **thirteenth move** is to give check with the white knight in the second square of the white king, and the black king will enter the fourth square of the white rook <term>roque: the rook or castle piece</term>.

¶ The **fourteenth move** is to give check with the white knight in the third square of the white bishop, and the black king will enter the fourth square of his rook.

¶ The **fifteenth move** is to give check with the white knight in the third square of the white knight; the black king will enter the third square of his knight.

¶ The **sixteenth move** is to give check with the white knight in the fourth square of the white rook. The white king <note>The text says "rey blaco" (white king), but the logic of the puzzle implies the Black King is being chased. This is likely a scribe's error.</note> will return by force to the second square of his knight, where he was first set up. In this way, the game is a **stalemate** <term>manna: a clever draw or forced technical outcome</term>.

And if one of the white knights fails to give check every single turn to the black king, the white king is mated instantly by the black rook, either by taking the white bishop that is in the white knight’s square or by taking the white pawn that is in the second square of the white rook. And this is the division of this game. And this is the figure of the setup:

<summary>This page describes a 16-move chess problem where White uses two knights to force the Black King on a journey across the board and back to its starting square. The game ends in a "manna" (a skillful draw or stalemate) if executed perfectly, but White loses immediately if the checking sequence is broken.</summary>

This is another **divided game** <term>iuego departido: a chess problem or endgame puzzle</term> in which there are twenty-nine moves <note>The text says "ueinte nueue" (29), though the description here only covers the first twelve.</note> that are to be **set up** <term>entablados: the specific arrangement of pieces on the board</term> exactly as shown in the illustration of the setup; and it is played in this way:

The black pieces play first. The **first move** is to give **check** <term>iaque: a move that threatens the King</term> with the black **rook** <term>Roque: the rook or castle</term> to the white King, who is on the square of that white rook; it is forced <note>Original: "fuerça que sea manna," meaning the King has no other legal move.</note>.

The **second move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the square of the white **knight** <term>cauallo: literally "horse"</term>; the white King must move to the third square of his **bishop** <term>alfil: historically the "elephant," the precursor to the modern bishop</term>.

The **third move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the third square of the white King; and the white King must move to the square of his **queen** <term>alfereça: the "alferza" or "vizier," the piece that preceded the modern queen, which moved only one square diagonally</term>.

The **fourth move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the square of the white King; the white King must move to the second square of his bishop.

The **fifth move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the square of the white bishop; the white King must move

to the third square of his knight.

The **sixth move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the third square of the white bishop; the white King must move to the fourth square of his rook.

The **seventh move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the third square of the white rook; the white King must move to the fourth square of the black knight.

The **eighth move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the fourth square of the black rook; and the white King must move to the third square of the black bishop.

The **ninth move** is to give check with this same black rook on the fourth square of the black bishop; the white King must move to the second square of the black knight.

The **tenth move** is to give check with this same black rook on the second square of the black bishop; the white King must move to the third square of the black rook.

The **eleventh move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the second square of the black rook; the King is forced to return to the fourth square of the black knight.

The **twelfth move** is to give check with this same rook, placed on the fourth square <note>Original: "q-ta," likely an abbreviation for "quarta."</note> of the black rook; the white King returns to the third square of his bishop: and in this [way it continues]...

The **first move** is to give check with the other white knight on the third square of the white bishop; and the black King will be forced <note>Original: "por fuerça."</note> to move to the fourth square of his queen.

The **second move** is to give check and **mate** with the white rook on the second square of the black queen.

<header>CHESS</header>

This is another partitioned game <term>uuego departido: a chess problem or puzzle with a specific starting arrangement</term> in which there are 36 games <note>The text likely refers to this being the 36th problem in a specific sequence within the manuscript</note> that are to be set up as shown in the illustration of the board: and they are to be played in this manner.

The black pieces play first, and they declare mate on the white King in four of their moves: in his fourth square. ¶ The first move: [black] shall give **check** <term>xaque: check</term> with the **rook** <term>roque: the rook or castle</term> placed there; it shall take the white **alfferza** <term>alfferza: the 'vizier,' precursor to the modern queen, which moved only one square diagonally</term> that is in the third square of the white alfferza; and the white King shall take it by force with his **alffil** <term>alffil: the 'elephant,' precursor to the modern bishop; it moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>. ¶ The second move: give check with the **knight** <term>cauallo: literally 'horse,' the knight</term> placed in the fourth square of the white King; and the white King shall enter the fourth square of his alfferza. ¶ The third move: give check with the **pawn** <term>peon: the foot soldier or pawn</term> placed in the fourth square of the king; and the white King shall take by force the knight that is in his fourth square. ¶ The fourth move: give check and mate with the other knight placed in the third square of the alfferza. ¶ And if the first players <note>the black side</note>, instead of giving check every time to the white King [fail to do so]: the King <note>referring here to the black king</note> is mated in two moves. ¶ The first is: give check with one

<column-break/>

white rook in the fourth square of the knight; and the King <note>the black king</note> must take it by force with his pawn. ¶ The second: give check and mate with the other white rook, taking this same pawn that took the white rook; and this is the explanation of this game: and this is the illustration of the board.

...in the second square of the white king, the white king enters the fourth square of his white ensign <term>alferez: the medieval equivalent of the queen, which at this time could only move one square diagonally</term>.

The eighth move <term>uuego: used here to denote an individual move or turn in the sequence</term>: give check <term>rraque: the medieval term for check, derived from the Persian 'shah'</term> with the black knight in the third square of the white bishop. The white king enters the fourth square of his bishop.

The ninth move: give check with the black rook <term>roque: the rook or castle</term> in the second square of the white bishop. The white king enters the third square of his knight.

The tenth move: give check with the black knight in the fourth square of the white ensign. The white king enters the fifth square of his rook.

The eleventh move: give check with the black pawn in the fourth square of the black knight. The white king enters the fourth square of the black rook.

The twelfth move: give check with the black knight in the third square of the black bishop. The white king will take the black pawn that is in the fourth square of the black knight.

The thirteenth move: give check with the black rook in the second square of the white knight. The white king enters the fourth square of the black bishop. If he moves into the fourth square of his own rook, checkmate <term>mate: from 'shah mat', meaning 'the king is defeated' or 'dead'</term> is made on the first move with the black rook in the fourth square of the white knight.

The fourteenth move: give check with the black knight in the third square of the black king. The white king enters the fourth square of the black ensign.

The fifteenth move: give check with the black rook in the fourth square of the black knight. The white king will take the black knight that is in the third square of the black king.

Its bishop turns with its white bishop: taking it with this same black rook: he makes mate in nineteen moves.

The sixteenth move: give check with the black bishop <term>alffil: from the Arabic 'al-fil', meaning 'the elephant', the precursor to the modern bishop</term> in its own square. The white king enters the second square of the black bishop.

The seventeenth move: give check with the black knight at the fourth square of the black king. The white king enters the square of the black knight.

The eighteenth move: give check and mate with the black bishop in the third square of the black king.

This is another set problem <term>uuego departido: a chess puzzle with a predetermined starting position and a specific goal</term> in which there are nine pairs of moves <term>nueue paries teleras: likely referring to a sequence of nine paired maneuvers or steps</term> that must be set on the board exactly as it appears in the illustration of the layout. And it is to be played in this manner:

The white pieces move first and give mate <term>mate: the final winning move</term> to the black king <term>rey preto: 'preto' is an older Spanish term for black</term> in ten <note>original: "x"</note> moves, neither more nor less, and the black pieces will not know how to delay it. ¶ The first move is to give check <term>xaque: a move that threatens the king</term> with the white rook <term>roque: the medieval chess piece, from the Persian 'rukh'</term> in the second square of the black rook, forcing the black king to move with his fers <term>alfferza: the predecessor of the modern queen, which could only move one square diagonally</term>. ¶ The second move is to give check with the white knight <term>cauallo: literally 'horse'</term> in the second square of the black bishop <term>alffil: the medieval bishop, which jumped exactly two squares diagonally</term>, and the black king shall enter into the square of his knight. ¶ The third move is to give check with the white rook in the square of the black bishop, and the black king will be forced to shield himself with his black bishop. ¶ The fourth move is to give check with the white knight in the third square of the black rook, and the black king shall return to the

<column-break/>

square of his rook. ¶ The fifth move: give check with the white rook and take <note>original: "madar," likely a scribe's error for "matar" or "mandar," meaning to take or capture</note> the black bishop that is in its square, and the black king will be forced to shield himself with his fers. ¶ The sixth move: give check with the white knight in the second square of the black bishop, and the black king shall enter into the second square of his rook. ¶ The seventh move: give check with the white bishop in the fourth square of the black bishop, and the black king shall enter into the third square of the black knight. ¶ The eighth move: give check with the white rook, capturing the black fers that is in the square of the black knight, and the black king shall enter into the fourth square of his rook. ¶ The ninth move: give check with the white rook in the square of the black rook; if the black king captures in the third square of his knight, it is mate on the next move <note>original: "al primero uuego," meaning the very next step in the sequence</note> by giving check with the white rook in the third square of the black rook; and because of this, it is better that he enters the fourth square of the white knight.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of chess problems (juegos departidos) found in the book. While the previous page concluded a 20-move problem, this page introduces a new, shorter problem requiring 18 moves to force a checkmate against the black king.</meta>

# CHESS

<note>A large rectangular miniature with a dark blue background framed in red and white. In the center, a vertical chessboard is displayed, showing a specific arrangement of white and black chess pieces. On the left side of the board, a noblewoman wearing a crown and a green gown sits on a bench, resting her hand on the shoulder of a kneeling female attendant dressed in grey. On the right side, another noblewoman in a crown and a red gown sits on a bench, likewise resting her hand on the shoulder of a kneeling female attendant in green. The scene depicts a chess problem (uuego departido) being studied.</note>

This is another chess problem <term>uuego departido: literally a "partitioned game," referring to a specific chess scenario or puzzle</term> in which there are eighteen moves that are to be set up <term>entablados: the starting arrangement of pieces on the board for a problem</term> just as it is shown in the illustration of the layout; it must be played in this manner.

The white pieces move first and give mate <term>mate: the end of the game where the king cannot escape</term> to the black King <note>original: "preto," an Old Spanish term for black</note> in eighteen moves or fewer. And this is their sequence of play, provided the black pieces do not know how to prolong it.

The first move <note>original: "uuego," meaning a single play or turn</note>: give check <term>raque: an archaic form of 'xaque' or 'check'</term> with the white knight <term>cauallo: literally 'horse'</term> in the second square of the black knight; and the black King enters into the second square of his bishop <term>alffil: the medieval ancestor of the modern bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>.

The second move: give check with the white rook <term>roque: the rook or castle</term> in the second square of the white bishop; and the black King enters the square of his knight.

The third move: reveal a check <note>original: "desubra raque," a discovered check</note> from the white rook by taking the black knight with the white knight in the third square of the black King. If the black King shields <note>original: "encastiere," to cover or block a check</note> himself with his knight, it will be the fourth move:

<column-break/>

to take this black knight with the white rook and give him check; and the black King enters the second square of his black rook.

The fifth move: give check and mate with the other white rook, placing it in the second square of the black bishop; or deliver the final mate <note>original: "ramarte," the concluding move</note> with the white bishop in the fourth square of the black bishop.

And if the white pieces fail to give check at every single turn from the first move: they are given check with his black rook in the second square of the white King or in the square of the white knight.

This is the distribution of this game and this is the figure of the arrangement that is painted here.

This is another chess problem <term>iuego repartido: literally a "divided" or "partitioned" game, known today as a chess problem or puzzle</term> in which there are twenty-two pieces <term>trebejos: an archaic Spanish term for chessmen or gaming pieces</term> that are to be set up <term>entablados: the specific initial arrangement of pieces for a problem</term> just as they are in the illustration of the layout; it is to be played in this fashion:

The white pieces move first <note>original: "p̄mo," abbreviation for 'primero'</note> and give checkmate <term>mate: the terminal position where a king cannot escape capture</term> to the black <note>original: "prieto," an Old Spanish word for black or dark</note> king in ten moves. And if they play in the square of the white rook <term>roque: the medieval rook, which functioned much like the modern one</term>—the one that is beside the black king—from the way they are now set up. And this is if the black king chooses to play his best to prolong the checkmate. ¶ The first move is to give check <term>xaque: the medieval term for 'check'</term> with the white rook in the square of the black bishop <term>alfil: the medieval ancestor of the bishop; in this era, it could only jump exactly two squares diagonally</term>. And the black king would move into the second square of his rook. ¶ The second move is to give check with this same white rook in the second square of the black bishop. And if the black king should take [the piece] in the square where he came from, which was his rook's square, give check with the white knight in the thi-

<column-break/>

-rd square of the black knight. And the black king would have to move into the square of his knight, and by giving check with the white rook, deliver checkmate in the second square of the black knight. And there the black king is mated in four moves. ¶ And therefore, the best thing is for the black king to be in the second square of the bishop when the white rook gives check to the black king in the second square of the black bishop; let the black king take the white pawn <note>original: "p̄o," abbreviation for 'peón'</note>. And that will be the third move. And give check with the white knight in the fourth <note>original: "qrta," abbreviation for 'quarta'</note> square of the black bishop. And the black king would move into the third square of his knight. ¶ The fourth move is to give check with the white rook in the second square of the black knight. And the black king would move into the third square of his bishop. ¶ The fifth <note>original: "q̄nto," abbreviation for 'quinto'</note> move is to give check with the white knight in the fourth square of the white fers <term>alferza: the precursor to the modern queen, but much weaker, moving only one square diagonally</term>. ...with the white counselor <term>alfferza: the medieval predecessor to the Queen, which could only move one square diagonally</term> in the second square, the black <note>original: "prieto," meaning dark or black</note> king will remain in his fourth square.

**The seventh move:** give check with the white knight in the third square of the black bishop, and the black king shall enter the third square of his bishop.

**The eighth move:** give check with the white rook in the second square of the black bishop, guarded by the white counselor. And the black king shall enter the third square of his knight.

**The ninth move:** give check with the white knight in the fourth square of the black king, and the black king shall enter the fourth square of his rook.

**The tenth move:** give check with the white pawn in the fourth square of the white knight. And the black king shall enter the fourth square of the white rook.

**The eleventh move:** give check with the white knight in the third square of the white bishop. And the black king must take the white pawn that is in the fourth square of the white knight.

**The twelfth move:** white shall give check with the white rook in the second square of the black knight <note>original: "cauallo blanco prieto," likely referring to the black side of the white knight's file</note>. If the black king enters the edge <note>original: "oreilla," meaning the border or edge of the board</note> at the fourth square of his rook, it is mate on the next move with the white rook in the fourth square of the black knight.

And therefore it is better for him to enter the fifth square of the white bishop.

**The thirteenth move:** give check with the white knight, taking the black pawn that is in the third square of the black king; and the black king shall enter the fourth square of the white king.

**The fourteenth move:** give check with the white rook in the fourth square of the white knight. And the black king will cover himself with his bishop to delay the mate.

**The fifteenth move:** give check with this same rook, taking the black bishop, and the black king must enter

<column-break/>

into the third square of the white counselor.

**The sixteenth move:** give check with the white bishop <term>alffil: the medieval bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term> from its own square. And the black king shall enter the second square of the white bishop.

**The seventeenth move:** give check with the white knight that is in the third square of the black king, placing it in the fourth square of the white counselor. And the black king shall enter the square of the white knight.

**The eighteenth move:** give check with the white bishop in the third square of the white counselor. And the black king must enter by force into the square of the white rook.

**The nineteenth move:** give check and mate with the white knight in the third square of the white knight.

And if the white pieces fail to give check every single time to the black king before the white side can move their white bishop or their white pawn that stands before the white king, the white king will be checkmated in this manner: they will give him check with the black knight in the third square of the white king. Then the white king will enter the square of his knight or his rook; it is better that he enters the square of his rook to delay the mate. And the check will leave the path <note>original: "raga," likely referring to the line of attack or the rank</note> open to the square of the white king.

And if the white king covers himself with his bishop, the black rook will take it and give check. And if he covers himself with his white knight, the rook will take that as well with that same check. And however much check is given to the white king, the white king will be taken by force. And they will give check and mate with the other rook, placing it in the square of the white king. Mate. Amen <note>original: "amenos," interpreted here as "amen" followed by a scribal flourish</note>.

And this is the explanation of this game, and this is the illustration of the layout.

This is another divided game <term>iuego repartido: a chess problem with a specific starting arrangement, common in the Arabic tradition as a 'mansuba'</term> in which there are twenty-one pieces <term>trebejo: a chess piece</term> that are to be arranged <term>entablados: set up on the board</term> just as it is in the illustration of the arrangement: and they are to be played in this way.

The Whites play first: and give mate to the black king in six of their own moves in the black square of his rook <term>roque: the medieval rook, which typically moved like the modern rook but was carved with two horn-like points</term>.

The first move: give check with the white bishop <term>alffil: the medieval bishop, which could only move exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term> in the fourth square of the black knight: and the black king shall enter the fourth square of the white king.

The second move: give check with the white pawn in the third square of the white queen <term>alfferza: the medieval equivalent of the queen, the 'vizier,' which only moved one square diagonally and was a much weaker piece than the modern queen</term>: and the black king shall enter the fourth square of his bishop.

The third move: give check with the white bishop in the second square of the black queen: and the black king shall enter the third square of his knight.

<column-break/>

black in the second square of his rook.

The fifth move: give check with the white bishop in the fourth square of the black bishop: and the black king shall enter by force into the black square of the black rook.

The sixth move: give checkmate with the white knight in the second square of the black bishop or in the third square of the black knight.

And if these Whites should fail to give check each time to the black king, then the black [king] mates the first [the white king]: by giving check with the black rook in the square of the white rook.

And this is the distribution <term>repartimiento: the specific layout or solution of a chess problem</term> of this game: and this is the illustration of the arrangement.

# BOOK OF THE [CHESS]

<note>A detailed manuscript illumination shows a chess problem. In the center, a large chessboard is shown vertically in a diagrammatic style. This is another divided game <term>uuego departtido: a chess problem or 'mansuba' with a set starting position</term> which contains specific conditions <term>articulos: points or technical requirements of the problem</term> that are to be arranged <term>entablados: set up on the board</term> just as it is in the illustration of the arrangement; it is to be played in this manner:

**The blacks play first** and give mate to the white king in seven of their own moves in the square of the black king.

¶ The first move: give check with the black knight <term>cauallo: knight</term> in the fourth square of the white king; from which he shall move to evade the check. Likewise, by giving the first check, the white king shall enter his third square; for he cannot shield himself with the knight because of the other check given by the black knight.

¶ The second move: give check with the black rook <term>roque: the medieval rook, which typically moved like the modern rook but was carved with two horn-like points</term> in the third square of the white bishop <term>alffil: the medieval bishop, which could only move exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>. The white king shall enter the fourth square of his queen <term>alfferza: the 'fers' or queen, which in this era moved only one square diagonally</term>.

¶ The third move: give check with the black queen in the fourth square of the black bishop. And the white king shall enter the fourth square of the black queen.

<column-break/>

¶ The fourth move: give check with the black promoted pawn <term>peon alfferzado: a pawn that has reached the eighth rank and been promoted to a queen/fers</term> by placing it in the third square of the black bishop. And the white king shall enter the third square of the black king.

¶ The fifth move: give check with the black bishop in its own square or in the fourth square of the white bishop; and the white king enters the second square of the lone king <term>rey seco: literally 'dry king', referring here to the opposing king stripped of its supporting pieces</term>.

¶ The sixth move: give check with the same black bishop in its own square or in the fourth square of the white bishop; and the white king enters the second square of the lone king.

¶ The seventh move: give check with the lone knight <note>original: 'cauallo seco'</note> in its third square; and the white king enters by force into the square of the lone king.

¶ The seventh move <note>The text lists two "seventh" moves; this final one is the actual delivery of mate</note>: give check and mate with the black rook in the square of the black bishop.

¶ If the blacks should make an error, the whites will not be able to give them mate in a fixed number of moves. However, the blacks would still be defeated <note>The text notes that if the sequence fails, White's superior number of pieces would eventually win the game through standard play</note>, for the whites have more pieces than they do. And this is the illustration of the arrangement.

# CHESS

<note>A rectangular framed illumination depicts a game of chess. Four figures are present: two men are seated on the ground or on low cushions, focused on a square chess board placed vertically between them to show the position of the pieces. The board has an 8x8 grid of alternating white and red squares. On the left, a player in a blue tunic and red hosen points toward the board. **This** is another divided game <term>uuego repartido: a chess problem or 'mansuba' with a set starting position</term> in which there are twenty-three games <note>original: "veint treze iogos"; this refers to its sequence in the manuscript's collection of problems</note> that are to be arranged <term>entablados: set up on the board</term> just as it is in the figure of the arrangement; and they are to be played in this manner. —
**THE** whites play first: and they give mate to the black king in five moves of the same games: if the blacks do not know how to delay it. **The** first move: give check with the white pawn <term>peon: pawn</term> in the second square of the black bishop <term>alfil: the medieval predecessor to the bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>. And if the black king shuts himself in the square of his rook <term>roque: rook</term>, it is mate in two moves. **The** first move: give check with that same white rook: in the square of the black bishop, and the black king will have to take [the piece] in the second square of his knight <term>cauallo: knight</term>. **The** second move: give check and mate with that same white rook in the square of the black knight. **And** therefore, the best [response] when the white rook gives check to the black king in the first move is for the black king to take that white rook with his

<column-break/>

queen <term>alferez: the 'ensign' or 'vizier,' a piece that moved only one square diagonally, later becoming the modern queen</term> at the opening <note>original: "a puerta"; likely meaning the queen takes the rook as it enters the king's defensive area</note>. **The** second move: give check with the white queen in the third square of the black bishop. And the black king enters the square of his rook. **The** third move: give check with the white pawn in the second square of the black knight. And the black king will take the white pawn that is in the second square of his king. **The** fourth move: give check with the white bishop in the fourth square of the black bishop. And the black king enters the square of his knight. **The** fifth move: give check and mate with the white knight in the third square of the black rook. **If** the whites fail to give check every time to the black king, the white king is checkmated: on the first move, by the check from the black rook in the second square of the white rook. **This** is the arrangement of this game: and this is the figure: of the layout.

-># BOOK OF THE [GAMES]<-

<note>A detailed miniature painting at the top of the page depicts three figures engaged in a game of chess. On the left, a man in a gold tunic and a woman in a grey tunic and red skirt are seated and gesturing towards a large chess board. On the right, another woman in a pinkish-orange gown is seated, observing the game. The scene is set under an architectural arcade with three trilobed arches, deep blue backgrounds with star-like motifs, and conical red-tiled roofs. The chessboard in the center displays a specific end-game problem with black and white pieces.</note>

This is another divided game <term>iuego departit: a chess problem or 'mansuba' with a set starting position</term> in which there are nineteen pieces <term>trebeios: chess pieces</term> that are to be set on the board just as they are in the illustration of the setup <term>entablamiento: the arrangement of pieces on the board</term>; and they are to be played in this way.

**The blacks play first and they give mate to the white king in six moves of their own games, or in fewer if the whites do not know how to delay it.**

The first move: give check with the black rook <term>roque: the medieval chess piece that became the modern rook</term> in the square of the white king. And the white king shall enter into the second square of his knight <term>cauallo: horse or knight</term>. The second move: give check with the knight placed in the fourth square of the white alfil <term>alffil: the predecessor to the bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>. If the white king should enter into the third square of his knight, it is mate in two moves. The first is to give check with the black rook in the third square of the white king; and the white king will enter by force into the fourth square of his rook. And the second move is to give mate with this same black rook in the third square of the white rook. And therefore, the best that he could

<column-break/>

play with the knight placed [to check] the white king in the fourth square of the white alfil, is that the white king play into the third square of his alfil. The third move: give check with the rook placed in the third square of the white king; and the white king shall enter into the fourth square of his alferza <term>alfferza: the predecessor to the queen, moving only one square diagonally</term>. The fourth move: give check with the black knight in the second square of the black alfil, holding the white king in the fourth square of the black alfil. The fifth move: give check with the black alferza in the third square of the black knight; and the white king will be forced to take the black pawn <note>original: "p'o lo prieto" likely an abbreviation for "peón prieto"</note>. The sixth move: give the "ruc" <note>original: "ruc", a Persian-derived term for the rook or the check itself</note> and mate with the rook placed in the second square of the black alfil. And if the blacks should fail to give check every single time to the white king, the king [white] is positioned to give mate on the first move with the white rook that is in the second square of the white alfil, by placing it in the second square of the white knight. And this is the division <term>repartimiento: the solution or distribution of moves</term> of this game. And this is the figure of the setup.

<note>Vocabulary used in this text: pieces (trebeios), setup (entablamiento), mate (mate), check (iaque), alfil (alffil), alferza (alfferza), rook (roque or ruc), knight (cauallo), black/dark (prieto), white (blanco), problem solution (repartimiento).</note>

**This is another chess problem <term>iuego departito: literally a "divided game," referring to a structured chess puzzle</term> arranged in four ways that are to be on the board just as in the illustration of the setup <term>entaulamiento: the layout of the pieces</term>; and they are to be played in this manner.**

The black pieces play first and give mate to the white king in four moves <term>vezes: literally "times" or "turns"</term>. And in these same games, if the white pieces do not know how to prolong the game, [the mate occurs even sooner].

The first move <term>iuego: here meaning a specific move or play</term> is check <term>xaque</term> with the black knight that is in the first square of the white bishop: [moving it] into the fourth square of the white counselor <term>alfferza: the medieval ancestor of the Queen, which could only move one square diagonally</term>. And if the white king enters the square of his counselor, it is mate on the very next move with the first black rook <term>roque</term>: in the square of the white bishop. Therefore, it is best for the white king to take the black knight with his white bishop <term>alfil: a medieval piece that moved by jumping exactly two squares diagonally</term> or with his white pawn.

The second move: check with the black rook in the second square of the white king; and if the white king does not want to take it with his white counselor, he will have to enter the square of his white counselor; and the third move will be check from the black pawn in the second square of the white bishop

<column-break/>

and the white king enters the square of his bishop.

The fourth move: check and mate with the black rook in the square of the white king; and in this way he mates the white king in four moves. And therefore it is best, in order to prolong the mate [beyond] four [moves], when the black rook checks the white king in his second square, that he takes it with his white counselor; the third move is check from the black knight in the second square of the white knight; and the white king will play into the square of his counselor.

The fourth move: check with the black pawn in the second square of the white bishop; and the white king enters the square of his bishop. And the fifth move: check with the other black pawn in the second square of the white knight; and the white king will have to take the black pawn that is in the second square of his bishop by force.

This other move: check with the black rook in the third square of the black bishop; if the white king enters the square of his white counselor, it is mate on the next move with the first black rook: in the square of the white bishop; and therefore it is best that he enters the square of his white knight.

## 29.

### CHESS GAME

...it is the least <note>This likely refers to the minimum number of squares or the most direct path allowed for the piece in this variation</note> that the black pawn <term>peon prieto: literally "dark pawn"</term> enters into the third square of the white king. ¶ The ninth move: give check <term>iaque: check</term> with the white bishop <term>alfil: the medieval precursor to the bishop, which could only move exactly two squares diagonally, jumping over other pieces</term> in its [own] square. And the black pawn shall enter the fourth square of the white queen <term>affersa: the medieval queen, a weak piece that moved only one square diagonally</term>. ¶ The tenth move: give check with the white rook <term>roque: rook</term> in the fourth square of the white bishop. And the black pawn shall enter into its fourth square. ¶ The eleventh move: give check with that same white rook in the fifth square of the white king. And the black pawn shall enter the third square of its queen. ¶ The twelfth move: give check with that same white rook in the sixth square of the black king. And the black pawn shall enter the second square of its bishop. ¶ The thirteenth move: give check with that same white rook in the third square of the black bishop. And the black pawn shall enter the square of its knight <term>cauallo: horse/knight</term>. ¶ The fourteenth move: give check with that same white rook in the square of the black bishop. And the black pawn shall enter by force into the second square of its rook. ¶ The fifteenth move: give check and mate <term>mate: checkmate</term> with that same white rook by placing it in the square of the black rook. ¶ Or, if the whites wish to give check each time to the black king, the white king is checkmated at the first move with one of the two black rooks in the second square of the black rook or in the third square of the white rook <note>The text suggests that if the white player deviates from the prescribed winning sequence, they risk being mated instantly by the black rooks, illustrating the precision required for the problem</note>. ¶ This is the division of this game, and this is the figure of the arrangement <term>entaulamiento: the initial setup of pieces for a specific chess problem or 'mansuba'</term>.
