This is another divided game <term>juego repartido: a chess problem with a specific starting arrangement and a set goal</term> in which there are eighteen <note>The OCR reads "v iij ueyntes," likely a transcription error for "xviij trebejos" (18 pieces), which is consistent with the complexity of these problems.</note> pieces that are to be set up just as they appear in the illustration of the arrangement; and they are to be played in this manner:
**The whites** play first and give mate to the black king <note>original: "rey preto"</note> in nine <note>The OCR reads "vij" (7), but the text describes moves up to the "noueno" (9th); "viiij" was the likely original number.</note> moves in the third house of the white king, which is where the white knight is placed.
The **first** move: they will give check <term>iaque: check</term> to the white king <note>The text says "white king," but in the context of White mating Black, this may refer to a move that forces the white king into a specific position or is a scribal error for the black king.</note> in the fourth house of his bishop <term>alfil: the medieval bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term> and the black king returns to the fourth house of his bishop. <gloss>¶</gloss> The **second** move: the white king will move into the house of the queen <term>alferza: the medieval queen, the weakest piece on the board, moving only one square diagonally</term> so that the black king enters into his second house. <gloss>¶</gloss> The **third** move: give check with the white knight in the fourth house of the queen so that the black king enters the second house of his bishop. <gloss>¶</gloss> The **fourth** move: give check with the other white knight in the fourth house of the black knight and the black king enters the third house of his knight. <gloss>¶</gloss> The **fifth** move: give check with the white king in the house of the black knight; and to prolong the mate, it is best that he

<column-break/>

takes it with his black bishop. And if the white king takes it with his bishop, as in the third move of the other sequence where the whites take this same black bishop, then the black king enters as he wishes into the fourth house of his rook <term>roque: the rook or castle</term> taking the white rook first with his bishop or withdrawing it. <gloss>¶</gloss> The **seventh** move: give check with the white knight in the third house of the black bishop, and the black king enters the fourth house of the white king. <gloss>¶</gloss> The **eighth** move: give check with the other white knight in the third house of the white bishop, and the black king enters the third house of the white rook. <gloss>¶</gloss> The **nine** move: give check and mate <note>original: "iaque z mate"</note> with the white bishop in its own house or in the fourth house of the black bishop. And in this game there is no other variation, except that the mate is made in the designated house. And this is the illustration of the arrangement that is painted here.

This is another **divided game** <term>juego repartido: a chess problem with a specific starting arrangement and a set goal</term> in which there are eighteen **pieces** <term>trebejos: an archaic Spanish term for chessmen</term> that are to be set up just as they appear in the illustration of the arrangement; and they are to be played in this manner.

The **whites** play first and give **mate** to the **black king** <note>original: "rey prieto"</note> in four moves, seeing the moves themselves; or in fewer, if the blacks do not know how to prolong it. The **first** move is to give **check** <term>iaque: the medieval spelling of "check," derived from the Persian word for "king"</term> with the white **rook** <term>roque: the ancestor of the modern rook, often depicted with two horn-like points</term> that is in the third house of the black **bishop** <term>alffil: from the Arabic "al-fil" (the elephant); in this era, the piece moved exactly two squares diagonally, leaping over other pieces</term>. The black bishop that is in its house takes it; or the black king is forced to take it with his black rook. The **second** move is to give check with the other white rook in the second house of the **fierce king** <note>original: "rey fero"; likely a scribal variant or a reference to a specific square on the king's side</note>. And if it enters the house of its **queen** <term>alffersa: from the Persian "farzin" (minister); at this time, the queen could only move one square diagonally and was the weakest piece</term>, it is mate at the first move while checking with the white **knight** in the second house of the black bishop, or in the third of the black king. First, it is bet-

<column-break/>

-ter that it be taken with its queen or with its knight. The **third** move is to give check with the white **pawn** in the second house of the black bishop, and the black king will enter the house of its queen. The **fourth** move is to give check and **mate** with the white knight in the third house of the black king. 

¶ If the whites want to give checkmates to the black king, the white king is [also vulnerable to] mate on the first move before the check with the black rook in the second house of the white king. 

¶ This is the arrangement of this game, and this is the illustration of the setup.

<note>A detailed miniature illustration is framed by three blue scalloped arches supported by white columns. The scene depicts two noblewomen seated on chairs, playing chess on a large 8x8 board. This is another **divided game** <term>juego repartido: a chess problem with a specific starting arrangement and a set goal</term> in which there are seventeen **pieces** <term>trevejos: an archaic Spanish term for chessmen</term> that are to be set up just as they appear in the illustration of the arrangement. And before all else, these games are played so that they may be won: first, they must deliver **mate** to the **white king** <note>original: "rey blanco"</note> in six moves, or in fewer for those who play it themselves, unless those who do not know how to play seek to prolong it.

¶ The **first** move is to give **check** <term>xaque: the medieval spelling of "check," derived from the Persian word for "king"</term> with the **rook** <term>roque: the ancestor of the modern rook, which moved the same way but was often shaped like a two-pronged fork</term> placed in the third house of the white king, and the white king shall enter into the second house of his **bishop** <term>alfil: the medieval precursor to the bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could leap over other pieces</term>.

¶ The **second** move is to give check with that same rook placed in the second house of the white king, and the white king shall enter the house of his **knight** <term>cauallo: literally "horse," referring to the knight piece</term>, and mate with this same rook placed in the second house of the white knight. This is because, at the very least, he is mated by the knight that is in the third house of the white knight.

¶ The **third** move is to give check with the

<column-break/>

same rook placed in the second house of the white knight, and the white king shall enter into the fourth house of his rook.

¶ The **fourth** move is to give check with the rook placed in the fourth house of the white knight, and the white king shall enter into the fourth house of the rook placed there.

¶ The **fifth** move is to give check with the **pawn** <term>peon: the foot soldier or pawn</term> placed in the third house of the **black knight** <note>original: "cauallo preto"</note> and the king shall enter into the third house of the **black rook** <note>original: "roque preto"</note>, taking the **black bishop** <note>original: "alfil preto"</note>.

¶ The **sixth** move is to give check and **mate** with the rook placed in the fourth house of the white rook. And although we have created another divided game that resembles this one, we commanded this one to be made because it is more beautiful to the eye; however, it cannot be won in fewer moves.

¶ This is the distribution of this game, and this is the illustration of the setup.

### PAGE 53.
### BOOK OF THE [GAMES]

<note>A large rectangular miniature is framed in red and gold. Inside, two women are seated on cushions, playing a game of chess. The woman on the left wears a blue hooded robe and is pointing towards the board. The woman on the right wears a pale pink robe and white wimple, holding one hand up in a gesture of contemplation. The background is a solid deep blue, and behind the board is a golden rectangular screen or wall section.</note>

**This is another divided game** <term>juego repartido: a chess problem with a specific starting arrangement</term> **in which there are seventeen pieces** <note>original: "dize siete uuegos," likely a scribal error for "diecisiete trevejos," meaning 17 pieces</note> **that are to be set up just as they appear in the illustration of the board arrangement.**

White wins in this manner: they move first and deliver **mate** <note>original: "mate"</note> to the **black king** <note>original: "prieto," literally meaning dark or black</note> in six moves, or in fewer if the players see it themselves, unless the black players know how to prolong it. 

¶ The **first** move is to give **check** <term>xaque: the medieval term for check</term> with the white **knight** <term>caballo: literally "horse," the piece that moves in an "L" shape</term> in the fourth **house** <term>casa: the medieval term for a square on the chessboard</term> of the black knight; and the black king shall enter into the third house of his **rook** <term>roque: the ancestor of the modern rook</term>. 

¶ The **second** move is to give check with the white rook in the house of the black rook; and the black king shall enter into the second house of his knight. 

¶ The **third** move is to give check with the white **pawn** <term>peón: the foot soldier piece</term> which is promoted to **queen** <term>alferza: the medieval predecessor to the queen, which moved only one square diagonally</term> in the house of the black **bishop** <term>alfil: the medieval predecessor to the bishop, which jumped exactly two squares diagonally</term>. And if the black king should take the white rook, give check with the white knight in the second house of the black bishop; and the black king shall enter into the second house of his rook. It is check—

<column-break/>

—and mate with the other white rook in the second house of the black knight. 

¶ The **fourth** move is the best way to prolong the mate: when the white pawn is promoted and gives check to the black king, the black king should not take the white rook, but rather move to the third house of his bishop. 

¶ Then the **fifth** <note>original: "dinto," a scribal error for "quinto"</note> move is check with the white rook in the third house of the black rook; and the black king enters into the fourth house of his bishop. 

¶ The **sixth** <note>The text here repeats "quinto" (fifth) in error before describing the final mate</note> move is to give check with the white pawn in the fourth house of the white king; and the black king enters into the fourth house of the white bishop. 

¶ The **final** move <note>original: "sexto"</note> is to give check and mate with the white knight in the third house of the white rook. And if the white pieces enter this way, they cannot be defeated in fewer moves. 

¶ This is the explanation of this game, and this is the illustration of the arrangement.

to move into the house of his **knight**.
¶ The **third** move is to give him **check**
and **mate** with the white **knight**, taking
the black **alfil** that is in the third

manner, the black king will be **mated** in three

in the second house of the black **alfil**,
and the black king will enter the second

the white king is **mated** on the very first
move by a **check** from the black **rook**
in the second house of the white **alfil**.
¶ This is the distribution of this
game, and this is the illustration of the
arrangement.

And if he <note>the black king</note> does not take it with his black alferza <term>alferza: the ancestor of the modern queen; in this era, it was a weak piece that moved only one square diagonally</term>, the black king would have to move into the square of his alferza, and it would be mate on the first move with the white knight <term>cauallo: the knight piece</term> in the second square of the black alfil <term>alfil: the ancestor of the modern bishop, which jumped exactly two squares diagonally</term>. <note>A detailed miniature illustration framed in red at the top of the folio depicts a chess problem from the "Book of Games." This is another partitioned game <note>a chess problem</note> of fifteen <term>chess: xedrez</term> pieces that are to be arranged on the board <term>entablados: the setup of pieces</term> just as it is shown in the illustration of the layout; and they are to be played in this manner:

¶ Those who play in this fashion <note>the dark pieces</note> play first and give mate to the white king in four moves or in fewer of those same moves, if the first players <note>the white side</note> do not know how to prolong it.

¶ The first move: give check with the black rook <term>roque: the rook piece</term> in the square of the white knight <term>cauallo: the knight piece</term>. And the white king will take the black alfil <term>alfil: the predecessor to the modern bishop, which at this time moved exactly two squares diagonally, jumping over other pieces</term> that is in the fourth square of the white alfil, just as if the black knight were there; he would be in mate with the other black rook in its square; or if he should block with his rook, one would take it with this same rook and give him mate, protected by the black knight.

¶ The second move: give check with the black knight that is in the third square of the black alfil. And place it in the fourth, which is a black check. And the white king will take it by force with his rook.

<column-break/>

The third move: give check with the black knight that is in the fourth square of the white rook. And place it in the third square of the black knight. And the white king will enter by force into the fourth square of the black alfil.

The fourth move: give check and mate with the black rook, placing it in the fourth square of the black alferza <term>alferza: the predecessor to the modern queen, moving only one square diagonally</term>.

And if the black pieces <note>original: "prietas"</note> fail to give check in their turn, or if they cannot defeat or give mate to one another in opposing moves, [the game is a draw]. And this is the distribution of this game.

¶ And this is the illustration of the layout that is painted here.

### This is another partitioned game <note>a chess problem</note> in which there are fifteen pieces <term>trebeios: chess pieces</term> that are to be arranged <term>entavladamiento: the setup of the pieces on the board</term> just as it is shown in the illustration of the layout; and it is to be played in this manner.

**W**hite plays first and gives mate to the black king in ten moves <term>lances: turns or moves in a chess sequence</term> of their own pieces in the square <term>casa: literally "house," referring to a square on the board</term> of the white king or in the square of the white alfil <term>alfil: the predecessor to the modern bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>. The first move: give check with the white knight <term>cauallo: knight</term> in the fourth square of the black alfil, with the black king being in the sixth square of his knight or in the fourth square. The second move: give check with the white rook <note>original: "iaque," a scribal variant for "roque"</note> in the second square of the black knight, with the black king being in the sixth square of his alfil. The third move: give white check in the fourth square of the black knight, with the black king moving to his third or his fourth square. The fourth move: give check with the other white rook in the second square of the black king, with the black king being in the fourth square of his fers <term>alffirza: the medieval equivalent of the queen, which moved only one square diagonally</term>. The fifth

<column-break/>

move: give check with the white knight in the third square of the white king where it was first placed. The black king moving to the fourth square of the white fers. The sixth move: give check with the white rook in the fourth square of the white king, or the black king takes <note>original: "main," likely a form of "takes" or "captures"</note> the white pawn that is in the third square of the white fers. The seventh move: give check with the white alfil in its own square, or in the fourth square of the black alfil, the black king moving to the second square of the white fers. The eighth move: give check from the fourth square of the white fers; if the black king should have his black rook, he will take it <note>the white piece</note> with that same rook and give check, the black king moving first, continuing with his rook or not, and moving into the square of the white king or the square of the white alfil. The tenth move: give mate with the white rook in the square of the white fers, guarded by the knight. And if the white side should fail to give each move correctly, they cannot win nor give mate to one another, even with the kings alone against each other. And this is the explanation of this game and this is the illustration of the layout.

<note>A large, framed manuscript miniature depicts two men seated on the ground, engaged in a game of chess. The man on the left has dark hair and a beard and wears a light greyish tunic. This is another partitioned game <note>original: "iuego departido"; a chess problem or endgame puzzle</note> in which there are eight pieces <term>iuegos: here referring to the pieces used in the problem</term> that are to be set up <term>entablados: arranged on the board</term> according to the figure of the layout, which are to be played in this manner:

The whites play first and give mate to the black king in four moves <term>vezes: turns or moves</term> or in fewer of their own moves if the blacks do not know how to delay it. ¶ The first move: give him check <term>xaq: check</term> with the white rook <term>roque: rook</term> that is in the square of its fers <term>alfferza: the fers, the predecessor to the modern queen, which moved only one square diagonally</term>, taking the black alfil <term>alfil: a piece that moved exactly two squares diagonally, jumping over others</term> that is in the third square of the black fers; and if the black king does not go to the second square of his knight <term>cauallo: knight</term> or the second square of his alfil, it is mate in three moves. ¶ The first move is check from the white rook in the second square of the black fers; the black king entering by force into the square of his knight or the square of his alfil. ¶ The second move: give mate with the white rook in the square of the black knight. Since it—

<column-break/>

—is better to delay the mate, he should enter the fourth square of his knight. ¶ The second move: check from that same white rook, taking the black rook <note>original: "roque prieto"</note> that is in the sixth square of the black knight, the black king entering into the fourth square of his rook or the fourth square of the white rook. ¶ The third move: check with that same white rook <note>The OCR says "roque prieto," but context implies the white rook is the one acting</note> taking the black pawn <term>peon: pawn</term> that is in the sixth square of the black rook. The black king will enter by force into the fourth square of his knight or the fourth square of the white knight. ¶ The fourth move: give mate with the other white rook in the sixth square of the black knight. And the blacks cannot avoid being defeated in at least these many moves. And this is the analysis <term>departimiento: the explanation or solution of the problem</term> of this game: and this is the figure of the layout.

This is another partitioned game <note>original: "uuego departit"; a chess problem or endgame puzzle</note> in which there are twenty-nine pieces <term>iuegos: here referring to the individual pieces on the board</term> that are to be set up <term>entau- lados: arranged or "tabled" on the board</term> just as it is shown in the figure of the layout. And the whites are to be played in this manner:

The whites play first and give mate to the black king <note>original: "rey prieto"</note> in four moves <term>uezes: turns or moves</term> or in fewer of their own moves if the blacks do not know how to delay it. <note>¶</note> The first move: a check <term>raque: an archaic form of "xaque," meaning check</term> from the white rook <term>roque: rook</term> with the white knight <term>cauallo: literally "horse"; the knight</term>, and let him place it in the second square of the black king. On the other hand, it is better whenever they resist the check with the white knight to prolong the

<column-break/>

mate, that the black king enters the square of his fers <term>alferza: the predecessor of the modern queen, moving only one square diagonally</term>. <note>¶</note> The second move: give the check with this same white rook in the square of the isolated alfil <note>original: "alfil seco"; likely referring to an alfil that is undefended or in a specific corner position</note> and the black king will take it by force with his alfil. <note>¶</note> The third move: give the check with the white knight in the same square of the isolated alfil, and the black king will enter his own square by force. <note>¶</note> The fourth move: check and mate with the white rook in the second square of the black king; if the turn to the black king is the white king <note>This indicates the white king is in a position to assist the mate</note>, if the whites start to give check on the turn to the black king, the white king [gives] mate on the first move with the fers placed, putting it in the same square of the white knight. And this is the division of this game, and this is the figure of the layout.

This is another partitioned game <note>original: "uuego departido"; a chess puzzle or endgame study with a predetermined setup</note> in which there are thirteen pieces <term>trebeios: chess pieces</term>: that are to be set up <term>entablados: arranged on the board</term> just as they are in the figure of the layout and they are to be played in this manner: 
The whites move: 
they play first and give 
mate to the black king <note>original: "rey prieto"; literally "dark king"</note> in 
five of their own 
moves in the 
square where he is stationed.

¶ The first move is to give check <note>original: "raque", a variant of "xaque" (check)</note> with the white rook <term>roque: the medieval chess rook</term> in the square of the black rook and take it from the black king by force. 

¶ The second move is to give a discovered check by placing the white bishop <term>alfil: the medieval predecessor to the bishop, which moved by jumping exactly two squares diagonally</term> in the fourth square of the black bishop, and the black king will move to the square of his knight <term>cauallo: knight</term>. 

¶ The third move is to give check with the white rook in the square of the black rook and take it from the black king by force. 

¶ The fourth move is to give check with the white pawn <term>peon: pawn</term> in the second square of the black knight; and the king will move

<column-break/>

to the square of his knight. 

¶ The fifth move is to give check and mate with the white knight in the third square of the black rook. 

¶ And the whites must give check every time to the black king: for if the white [player] does not give mate on the first move, the black [player] will give check with his own rook that is in the second square of the white knight, placing it in the square of the white knight or in the second square of the white rook. 

¶ This is the explanation of this game, and this is the figure of the layout.

# FOLIO XL

<note>A large miniature illustration from the 13th-century "Book of Games" (Libro de los Juegos) by Alfonso X. This is another **arranged game** <term>uuego departtido: a pre-set chess problem or endgame puzzle</term> in which there are pieces <note>original: "riesto," which in this context likely refers to the "remaining" pieces or the specific "set" of ten pieces mentioned in the previous problem</note> that are to be **set up** <term>enttablados: arranged on the board according to the diagram</term> just as it is shown in the figure of the layout. and they are to be played in this manner:

The **black pieces** <term>prietos: literally "dark ones"</term> move first: they give **mate** to the white king in four moves or in fewer, in all their moves, if the white pieces do not know how to prolong it. The first move is to give **check** <term>raque: an archaic form of the modern "jaque"</term> with the **pawn** <term>peon</term> placed in the third house of the white **knight** <term>cauallo</term>. And the white king will move to the house of his knight; or if he covers himself in the house of his **rook** <term>roque</term>, he will be mated on the first move with the rook placed in the house of the white knight. The second move is to give check with the rook placed in the house of the white knight, and the white king will cover with his **bishop** <term>alffil: from the Arabic "al-fil," meaning elephant</term>. The third move is to give check with the rook that is first in its house...

<column-break/>

...in the house of the white rook, and the white king must take it by force. The fourth move is to give check and **mate** <term>matthe</term> with the rook by capturing the white bishop that is in its house. And if the black pieces should fail to give check in this way to the white king, the [black] king is put in checkmate on the first move with the white rook by placing it in the second house of the black bishop. And this is the **explanation** <term>departtimientto</term> of this game, and this is the figure of the layout.

->GAMES<-

<note>An architectural frame with three trefoil arches contains a scene of two men playing a board game. The frame is blue with red and white decorative patterns. This is another **arranged game** <term>uuego repartido: a pre-set chess problem or endgame puzzle</term> in which there are ten pieces <note>original: "diez aciertos," likely referring to the ten pieces remaining on the board</note> that are to be **set up** <term>entablados: arranged on the board</term> just as it is shown in the figure of the **layout**. It is played from this position and in two ways <note>original: "dos puntos," referring to two possible lines of play</note> that will arrange for the black pieces first to give **mate** to the **white king** in five moves or in fewer; all the moves are the same if the white players do not know how to delay it. ¶ The **first move** is to give mate with the **pawn** placed in the third house of the white **rook**; if the white king enters the house of his rook, it is mate on the first move with the rook placed in the house of the white **alfil** <term>the medieval predecessor to the bishop, which jumped exactly two squares diagonally</term>. And through the middle is better than entering the house of his **knight**. ¶ The **second move** is to give **check** <term>rraque: a variation of "xaque" or check</term> with this same pawn placed in the second house of the white rook. If the white king enters the house of his rook, the rook will give a direct check <note>original: "roq' seco," likely meaning a check delivered by the rook alone without support</note> in the house of the white alfil, and the white king will have to enter the second house of his knight. And that one will give

<column-break/>

**mate** with this same direct rook in the house of the white knight. For this reason, it is better to delay the mate than not to, because the pawn will be at the white king in the second house of the white rook, so that the white king comes to the second house of his knight. ¶ The **third move** is to give check with the first rook in the second house of the white alfil so that the white king enters the house of his rook. ¶ The **fourth move** is to give check with that same rook in the house of the white alfil, and the white king will enter by force into the second house of his knight. ¶ The **fifth move** is to give mate with this same direct rook in the house of the white knight. ¶ And if the turns <note>original: "suertez," referring to the luck of the dice or the sequence of play</note> should result in giving check in reverse to the white king, the **dark king** <term>rey prieto: the black king</term> is mated on the first move with the white rook that is in the third house of the placed rook, by putting it in the house of the placed rook. ¶ This is the **arrangement** of this game, and this is the figure of the layout.

### BOOK OF [GAMES]

<note>A large miniature at the top of the page depicts a game of chess set within an architectural framework of five blue polylobed arches with golden finials. Two men are seated on a red-striped bench. The man on the left has light hair and a beard, wearing a white tunic and a reddish-orange mantle; he gestures toward the board. The man on the right has a white beard and wears a white tunic and a white Phrygian-style cap <note>The Phrygian cap often denoted Eastern or scholarly status in 13th-century Spanish art.</note>; he holds a document or book featuring several rows of Arabic numerals including 3, 4, 5, 2, and 6. Between them is a large vertical chessboard with red and white squares, displaying an endgame position (a chess problem) with various pieces including kings, rooks, a knight, and pawns.</note>

This is another **arranged game** <term>iuego repartido: a pre-set chess problem or endgame puzzle</term> in which there are ten **pieces** <term>trebejos: chessmen or pieces</term> which are to be **set up** <term>entablados: arranged on the board</term> just as they are in the figure of the **layout** <term>entablamiento: the diagram of the starting position</term>; and they play in this way:

The **black pieces** <term>prietos: literally "the dark ones," referring here to the black side</term> play first: and they give **mate** to the **white King** in three of their own moves in the third **square** <term>casa: literally "house," the standard term for a square on the board</term> of the black **Rook** <term>Roque: the medieval rook, which moved exactly as the modern rook does</term> which is set on a white square.

The first move that is played: [they] give check <note>original: "da que," a likely scribal or OCR error for "da xaque" (gives check)</note> with a black Rook together with the black **Bishop** <term>alffil: the medieval ancestor of the bishop, which jumped diagonally exactly two squares</term>, placing it in the third square of the black King; and the white King enters the second square of the black Rook.

The second move: give him check with the other black Rook in the second square of the black **Knight** <term>cauallo: literally "horse"</term>; and the white King enters the third square of the black Rook.

The third move: give him check and mate with the other black Rook in the square of the black Rook.

And if the black pieces should fail to give check

<column-break/>

every time to the white King: the black King is mated on the very first move by a single check from either of the two white Rooks in the square of the white Rook or the white Knight.

This is the **arrangement** of this game: and this is the figure of the **layout**.

<summary>This page describes a chess problem where the black pieces must achieve checkmate in three moves using a combination of rooks and a bishop, or face immediate defeat if they fail to maintain the check.</summary>

# HAIL THE KING <note>original: "AUE OREX," a variant of the Latin "AVE REX"</note>

<note>A detailed illumination within an architectural frame of five gothic arches supported by slender pillars. Beneath the arches, two figures sit on a long, patterned bench. Both figures appear to be scholars or clerics, wearing robes and miters (one in white, one in blue and white). They are seated on opposite sides of a square chessboard positioned in the center. This is another **arranged game** <term>iuego departido: a pre-set chess problem or endgame puzzle</term> in which there are ten pieces that are to be **set up** <term>entablados: arranged on the board</term> just as they are in the figure of the **layout** <term>entablamiento: the diagram of the starting position</term>; and they are played in this way:

The **black pieces** <term>prietos: literally "the dark ones," referring here to the black side</term> play first and give **mate** to the **white King** in three of their own moves; and the fourth is that of the white **bishop** <term>alfil: in medieval chess, the bishop moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>.

<margin>In.</margin> The first move is to give **check** <term>xaque: a direct threat to the king</term> with the black **knight** <term>cauallo: literally "horse"</term> in the fourth **square** <term>caſa: literally "house," the standard term for a board space</term> of the white knight, and the white King must take it by force with his white **rook** <term>roque: the tower piece</term>. The second move is to give check with the black rook that is in the square of the black bishop, placing it in the third square of the white bishop, and the white King will take it by force. The third move is to give check and mate with the black rook in the third square of the black **ensign** <term>alferez: the medieval equivalent of the queen, though much weaker, moving only one square diagonally</term>. And if the black pieces should fail to give check or if they **change** <note>original: "camuies," meaning to vary or fail the sequence</note>

<column-break/>

their moves toward the white King, the black King is **mated** on the first move by giving him check with the white rook that is in the second square of the black rook, placing it in the square of the black rook. Or with the other white rook, placing it in the square of the black knight. And this is the **explanation** <term>departimiento: the solution or breakdown of the problem</term> of this game; and this is the figure of the layout.

# CHESS

<note>A large rectangular illustration occupies the top half of the page, framed by an architectural structure featuring three trilobed arches with blue and patterned backgrounds. This is another **arranged game** <term>iuego departido: a chess problem or puzzle starting from a specific position</term> in which there are eighteen **pieces** <term>arbiçios: a term used in this manuscript for game pieces, likely derived from Arabic terminology</term> that are to be **arranged on the board** <term>entablados: set up according to a diagram</term> just as it appears in the illustration of the layout; and it is played in this way:
**T**he white pieces play first: they give **mate** <term>mate: checkmate</term> to the black king in three moves by their own play, or in fewer if the black pieces do not know how to position themselves. **T**he first move will give **check** <term>raque: an archaic form of 'xaque' or check</term> with the white **knight** <term>cauallo: horse</term> that is on the fourth square of the white **bishop** <term>alffil: the medieval bishop, which moved differently than the modern version, jumping to the second square diagonally</term>, placing it on the third square of the **black queen** <note>original: "alfferza petri," likely a transcription of "alfferza prieta"</note> <term>alfferza: the 'general' or queen, which in this period moved only one square diagonally</term>. If the black king enters the square of his queen, it is mate on the first move with the white **rook** <term>Roque: the castle or rook</term> on the second square of the black queen, or with the white **pawn** <term>peon</term> on the second square of the white bishop. Therefore, it is best for him to enter the square of his knight. **T**he second move will give check with the white rook on the second square of the white knight; and the black king will enter the square of

<column-break/>

his rook by necessity. **T**he third move will give checkmate with the other white knight on the third square of the black knight. **A**nd if the white pieces **fail** <note>original: "enveren," likely a transcription for "errasen," to err or fail</note> to give check to the black king every time, the white king is checkmated on the first move by giving check with the black rook that is on the square of the white bishop, placing it on the square of the white king. **A**nd this is the **solution** <term>apuntamiento: the explanation or "pointing out" of the correct play</term> to this game, and this is the figure of the layout.

This is another **problem** <term>uego apartido: literally a "divided game," referring to a chess puzzle or endgame scenario</term> in which there are 15 moves <note>The text says "15 games," but in this context, it refers to the variations or moves required to solve the puzzle</note> that are to be **set up** <term>entablados: positioned on the board</term> just as it is shown in the figure of the layout; and they are to be played in this manner.—
**T**his is another problem in which there are 15 moves that are to be set up just as it is shown in the figure of the layout; and they are to be played in this manner.—
**W**hite plays **first** <note>original: "primo"</note> and they give mate to the black king in six moves, or in less than five of those same moves if the black pieces do not know how to prolong it.

<column-break/>
square of the black knight. If the white rook checks the black king by taking the black knight, and the black king enters the square of his queen, the sixth move is check from the white knight in the third square of the black bishop. If the black king plays in his own square, it is mate with the white rook in the square of the black knight. If he plays it in the square of his bishop, it is likewise mate with the white queen in the second square of the black queen. And if the white pieces [fail] and the king checks the black king, the game is lost; mate on the first move with the black rook that is in the second square of the white knight—place it in the second square of the white rook—or with the other black rook, place it in its own square.

This is another **problem** <term>iuego repartido: literally a "divided game," referring to a chess puzzle or endgame scenario with a specific setup</term> in which there are twelve **chessmen** <term>axedreces: chess pieces</term> that are to be **arranged** <term>entablados: placed on the board</term> just as it is in the illustration of the layout; and it is to be played thus: The **black** <term>prietos: literally "dark ones," referring to the black pieces</term> move and give **checkmate** <term>mate: the end of the game</term> to the white king in ten moves from these same positions, in the fourth square of the black rook, which is set up in the sixth house <note>original: "caſa ſena"</note>.

¶ The first move: that black **rook** <term>roque: from the Persian 'rukh'</term> gives check in the second square of the white **bishop** <term>alffil: the predecessor to the modern bishop, which moved by leaping exactly two squares diagonally</term>, and the white king is held in his square. ¶ The second move: that black **knight** <term>cauallo: literally "horse"</term> gives check in the third square of the white **queen** <term>alfferza: from the Persian 'al-farzin,' the counselor; the predecessor to the modern queen, moving only one square diagonally</term>, and the white king is held in his bishop's square. ¶ The third move: that black rook gives check in the second square of the white bishop, and the white king is held in his knight's square. ¶ The fourth move: that black **pawn** <term>peon: literally "foot soldier"</term> gives check in the second square of the rook from its starting position. ¶ The fifth move: that black rook gives check in the square of the white bishop, and the white king is held in the second square of his knight. ¶ The sixth move: that rook...

<column-break/>

...black gives check in the white knight's square, and the white king is held in the third square of his house. ¶ The seventh move: that black knight gives check in the second square of the white bishop, and the white king shall move to the fourth square of his rook. ¶ The eighth move: that black rook gives check in the fourth square of the white knight, and the white king is forced into the fourth square of his own rook. ¶ The ninth move: that black pawn gives check in the third square of the black knight to take the white pawn, and they give check to the black king <note>original: "dān daq̄ al rey prieto"; this describes a move where the black king is placed in check by the white pieces as part of the sequence</note>. ¶ The tenth move: check and mate to the white king with the other black pawn, taking the white pawn that gave check to the black king.

¶ And if the black pieces fail to give check every single time to the white king, then the black king is mated on the first move by the white rook placed in the house of the black bishop. ¶ And this is the distribution of this game, and this is the illustration of the layout.

This is another **chess problem** <term>uuego departido: literally a "divided game," referring to a structured endgame puzzle with a set solution</term> in which there are certain pieces that must be arranged <note>original: "entablados"</note> just as they are in the figure of the layout <note>original: "entablamiento"</note>. And the **white pieces** are to be played in such a way that they first give **mate** to the **dark king** <note>original: "Rey prieto"</note> in seven of their own moves. And the **white queen** <term>alfierza: the medieval precursor to the modern queen, which was a much weaker piece that could only move one square diagonally</term> and the **promoted pawn** <note>original: "peon alfierzado," a pawn that has reached the eighth rank and become a queen</note> are guarded. And the dark king cannot move himself so long as he has a square where he can enter <note>This implies the King is immobilized or that other legal moves for his side exist.</note>.

¶ The **first move**: place the white promoted pawn in the second square of the dark **bishop** <term>alffil: the medieval bishop, which moved exactly two squares diagonally and could jump over other pieces</term>. And the dark king has no square in which he can play; therefore, he is forced to play his dark pawn that is in the fourth square of his dark bishop.

¶ The **second move**: place the promoted pawn in the square of the dark queen. And the dark king will enter by force into the square of his dark **knight** <note>original: "cauallo"</note>.

¶ The **third move**: place the white queen in the second square

<column-break/>

of the dark knight; and the dark king will enter by force into the second square of the dark **rook** <term>roque: the medieval term for the rook or castle</term>.

¶ The **fourth move**: place the promoted white pawn in the second square of the dark bishop. And the dark king cannot move himself; therefore, he must play his dark pawn into the third square of the white bishop.

¶ The **fifth move**: place the white queen in the third square of the dark rook. And the dark king enters by force into the square of his dark rook.

¶ The **sixth move**: place the white promoted pawn in the square of the dark knight. And the dark king has no square where he can enter; and it is necessary that he plays by force his dark pawn into the second square of the white bishop.

¶ The **seventh move**: **check and mate** <note>original: "iaque 7 mate"</note> with the white queen in the second square of the dark knight.

# BOOK OF THE

<note>A large rectangular illustration is set within a Gothic architectural frame featuring three blue-vaulted arches with red and white patterns above. In the central space, a large chessboard with alternating red and tan squares sits on a low platform. Two figures are seated on either side of the board: on the left, a figure in white robes and a flat white hat points toward the board; on the right, a woman in a long red and white gown with a white head covering looks at the game. Several chess pieces—rendered in the medieval "shatranj" style—are positioned on the board. Below the image, the text is arranged in two columns, beginning with a large decorative blue initial 'E' featuring red penwork flourishes.</note>

**E**his is another **chess problem** <term>uuego departid: literally a "divided game," referring to a structured endgame puzzle with a set solution</term> in which there are eleven pieces <note>original: "uerros," likely a variant of pieces or points</note> that are to be set up <note>original: "entablar"</note> just as they are in the figure of the layout. And they are to be played in this manner: The **white pieces** shall play first, and they must give **check** <term>iaque</term> to the **black king** <note>original: "rey prieto"</note> in exactly three of their moves—neither more nor less—unless the black side knows how to delay it. And if they know how to delay it, the white pieces remain defeated. The **first move** is to place the **white pawn** <note>original: "peon"</note> in the second square of the **black bishop** <term>alfil: in medieval chess, the bishop was a weaker piece that moved exactly two squares diagonally, jumping over other pieces</term>, giving check to the black king. And if the black king enters the square of his own bishop, it is **checkmate** <term>mate a las tres lanzas: literally "mate at the three lances," a phrase for a direct three-move win</term> in this way: The **second move** is to place the **white bishop** in the fourth square of the other black bishop against the black king. And he cannot move anything of his own, nor can his **rooks** <term>roque: the medieval rook, which moved the same as the modern rook</term> do anything, so that it becomes checkmate on the **third move** with the **white pawn**, placing it in the second

<column-break/>

square of the **black knight** <note>original: "cauallo"</note> or with the **white knight**, giving such check and mate to the black king, or a *mate mart* <note>original: "mate mart," a medieval term for a specific type of checkmate, possibly related to "deadly mate"</note> with the white knight. And therefore, it is better for the black pieces—when the white side plays against the black king with the white pawn in the second square of the black bishop—that the black king enters the square of his rook. And the best move the white pieces can then make is to place the **white bishop** in its sixth square; this will be the **second move**. And to escape the checkmate, the black king must play with his rook which is in the second square of the white rook, placing it in the third square of the designated rook and taking the white pawn that is there. And in this way, the white pieces will be defeated <note>Contextually, "vencerán los blancos" here implies they "remain defeated" or lose the challenge because they failed the three-move requirement.</note> because they cannot give mate to the black king in the three moves mentioned above, and the plan is disrupted <note>original: "desatize"</note>. And this is the **skill** <term>artaria: the "art" or strategic secret</term> of this game.

# CHESS OF THE KING

<note>A large rectangular illustration occupies the top half of the page, set under a blue architectural canopy with three gothic arches supported by slender columns. This is another **chess problem** <term>uuego repartido: literally a "divided game," referring to a structured endgame puzzle with a set solution</term> in seventeen pieces <note>original: "azitios," likely referring to the pieces or positions on the board</note> that are to be laid out just as they are in the illustration of the layout <note>original: "encablamiento"</note>; and they are to be played in this manner.
**It** is set so that the [black pieces] move first, and in such a way that they **checkmate** <term>mate</term> the **white king** in eight of their own moves, neither more nor less. And they are played in this manner: **Check** <term>xaque</term> with the **black pawn** <note>original: "peon prieto"</note> that is in the third square of the **white bishop** <term>alffil: in medieval chess, the bishop moved exactly two squares diagonally, jumping over other pieces</term>, placing it in the second square of this same bishop. And the white king shall move by force into the square of the **white rook** <term>roque</term>. ¶ The **second move**: the black [pieces] shall play with their black pawn that gave check to the king in the square of the white bishop, making it a **queen** <term>alfferza: in this era, the queen or "ferz" was a weaker piece that moved only one square diagonally</term>, and the white king shall move by force into the square of his **white knight** <term>cauallo</term>. ¶ The **third move**: the black [pieces] shall play with their promoted pawn <note>original: "peon alfferzado"</note> into the third square of the white rook, capturing it <note>original: "descomiendol"</note>. And the white king shall move by force

<column-break/>

into the square of his same rook. ¶ The **fourth move**: the black [pieces] shall play with their knight that is in the third square of the white **queen** <note>original: "alfferz"</note>, placing it in the square of the white bishop. And the white king shall move into the square of his white knight by force. ¶ The **fifth move**: the black [pieces] shall play with their knight into the second square of the white king, and the white king shall move by force into the square of the white rook. ¶ The **sixth move**: the black [pieces] shall play with their black bishop that is in the fourth square of the black knight, placing it in the third square of the white king. And the white king shall move into the square of his knight if he wishes, or he shall play with his white pawn that is in the fourth square of the white knight, placing it in the fourth square of the black knight. ¶ The **seventh move**: the black [pieces] shall play with their knight into the third square of the white bishop. And the white king shall move by the force of his white pawn <note>indicating the movement is constrained by the pawn's position</note>. ¶ The **eighth move**: give him check and **mate** with the black pawn that is in the third square.

...to give his king mate with a check. The white **promoted pawn** <note>Original: *pon alffersado*. Even if he wished, he cannot place himself in any spot where he does not lose. And in this way, the blacks are defeated. ¶ This is the **explanation** <note>Original: *departimiento*.</note> of this game, and this is the illustration of the **layout** <note>Original: *entablamiento*.</note>.

-># BOOK OF DICE<-

<note>A large horizontal miniature at the top of the page depicts a scene from the court of King Alfonso X of Castile. ### In what manner dice should be made

Since we have spoken as fully as we could of the games of **chess** <note>original: *agedrez/acedrez*</note> which are played by <term>skill</term> <note>original: *seso*, referring to logic and intellectual strategy</note>; we wish now to tell here of the games of dice for two reasons. The first is because the agreement of the wise men—as we showed at the beginning of the book—was to determine which was better: skill or <term>chance</term> <note>original: *uentura*, meaning luck, fate, or fortune</note>. And regarding this, each one saw his proof. ¶ The first regarding skill, through the games of chess. ¶ The second regarding chance, through the dice. ¶ The other reason is that, although **tables** <note>original: *tablas*, the family of games including backgammon</note> are more subject to absolute chance than dice, because they cannot be played without them, it was necessary that we speak of the dice first.

<column-break/>

And we say that they must be three square figures with six equal sides, each one of the same size as the other in magnitude and in the equality of the square. For if it were otherwise, one side would not fall as well as another; and it would be a matter of deception rather than chance. ¶ And this is one of the ways of cheating—as we shall describe later—which very deceitful people use when they wish to cheat with them. ¶ And there must be on these six faces, in each one of them, points placed in this manner: ¶ On one six; on another five; on another four; on another three; on another two; and on the other one. Thus, there shall be twenty-one points on each die, so that in all three dice there are sixty-three points. ¶ And the points must be placed in this manner: beneath the face of the six, the <term>ace</term> <note>original: *as*, the one</note>; beneath the five, the two; and beneath the four, the three <note>original: *tri*</note>. ¶ And these dice can be made...

<summary>This page introduces the section on dice games, explaining that dice represent pure chance in the philosophical debate between luck and skill. It provides technical specifications for making fair dice, including the arrangement of opposite faces.</summary>
<keywords>dice, Alfonso X, chance, skill, chess, backgammon, cheating, geometry, Castile, medieval games</keywords>

->The game of Highs: and how it is played<-

The first and most common game of those used by men is that he who throws the most **points** <note>original: *puntos*</note> wins; and this game they call <term>Highs</term> <note>original: *mayores*</note>. ¶ Regarding the other ways of playing that come from them, these are variations <note>original: *posturas*, literally "placements" or "bets"</note> that men established in such a way that they are <term>games of chance</term> <note>original: *iuegos de baratos*; in this context, *baratos* refers to the "rake" or the gambling house's cut, or games played for quick stakes.</note>. ¶ Such as if the one who throws the fewest points were to win. ¶ Likewise, it is the same in this one as in the two [others], which is in

<column-break/>

this manner: that if a six is shown on one die, one says for the other two: five and ace, or four and two, or two threes <note>original: *ternas*</note>. ¶ And if a five is shown on one: one says for the other two: four and ace, or three and two. ¶ And if a four is shown on one: one says for the other two: three and ace, or two twos. ¶ And if a three is shown on another: one says for the other two: two and ace. ¶ And if a two is shown on another: one says for the other two: double aces.

### This is the game of Triga.

**A**nother game exists that they call <term>Triga</term> <note>Triga likely derives from the Latin *tri-*, referring to the three dice used, or the "triples" that serve as automatic wins.</note> which is played in this manner: if a man plays with others, he first throws triples <note>original: *par en los tres dados*, literally a "pair across the three dice," meaning all three match.</note> on the three dice, or fifteen points, or sixteen, or seventeen, or eighteen. Or the remainder of these <note>The low counterparts to the high winning numbers.</note> which are six, five, four, and three, which win. And these are all called <term>winning combinations</term> <note>original: *fuertes*</note>.

¶ Triga and these results can occur in this way: ¶ the eighteen points are **sixes on all three** <note>original: *Senas al tres*</note>. The seventeen are **two sixes and a five**. ¶ The sixteen are **two sixes and a four**, or **two fives and a six**. ¶ The fifteen are **two sixes and a three**, or **a six, a five, and a four**, or **fives on all three**.

<column-break/>

**L**ikewise, the other totals by which it is played: six points can occur in this way: **a four and two aces**, or **a three, a two, and an ace**, or **three twos**. ¶ The five points: **a three and two aces**, or **a two, a two, and an ace**. ¶ The four points: **a two and two aces**. ¶ The three points: **aces on all three**. ¶ Likewise, matching numbers on all the dice can occur in this way: **sixes on all three**, or **fives on all three**, or **fours on all three**, **threes on all three**, **twos on all three**, or **aces on all three**. In no other way can it be played <note>Referring to these specific "strong" or automatic winning throws.</note>.

**T**here is another way <insert>to play:</insert>
in which a man might throw the dice and none of these winning combinations that we have mentioned here appear, yet it shall still be a **Triga**. This is if a man takes as his mark <note>original: *m.*, likely an abbreviation for *marco*, meaning a target number or "point."</note> seven points, or eight, or nine, or ten, or eleven, or twelve, or thirteen, or fourteen. ¶ And in this way, he who plays shall throw that same...

# OF TWO [DICE]

### The game called Azar

Another type of dice game exists which they call <term>azar</term> <note>From the Arabic *al-zahr* (the die). In this specific game, it refers to both the game itself and to specific "lucky" or "unlucky" winning rolls.</note>, and it is played in this manner. The one who attempts to roll must try to throw the highest counts: sixteen <note>original: *.vj. puntos o dize seys*. In medieval shorthand, "dize seys" often refers to *diez y seis* (sixteen). In this game, 16, 17, and 18 are the winning "azares" for the caster.</note>, seventeen, or eighteen. Or, he may throw the <term>lobrigas</term> <note>original: *lobrigas*. This literally means "dark" or "gloomy" rolls; in dice games, these are the low, losing totals.</note> of these luck-rolls, which are six, five, four, or three: [with these] he wins. ¶ And whichever of these luck-rolls appears, in whatever combination they may come according to the other games we shall describe below, it is called an *azar*. ¶ And if by chance he does not throw any of these *azares* on the first roll: he takes another total by chance—combinations that are from seven points and up, or fourteen and below <note>original: *seis puntos a arriba o de quinze ayuso*. In *Azar*, if you don't roll a winning or losing "azar" (3, 4, 5, 6 or 16, 17, 18), you set a "point" or "chance" between 7 and 14.</note>—in whichever manner it may come, as we said of the other games. ¶ And after these, if he should throw any of the luck-rolls that

<column-break/>

we say are *azar*, this roll shall be called a <term>reazar</term> <note>A "counter-azar." If the first player sets a "point" and the second player then rolls an *azar* number, it challenges the first player's position.</note> on behalf of the one who first rolled. ¶ And likewise, if by chance he does not throw this roll that is taken as a *reazar*, he takes for himself one of the other luck-rolls that are from seven points and up or fourteen and below, in whichever manner it may come. ¶ And it shall be that they roll as many times as necessary until one of these rolls appears: either his own, by which he wins, or the other player's, by which he loses. The only exception is if he should roll that same total that the other player saw [rolled]: which would then be called an <term>encuentro</term> <note>original: *encuentro*. Meaning an "encounter" or "clash," occurring when a player rolls the exact number their opponent needs.</note>. ¶ And it is [required] that they return to rolling as if starting anew. ¶ And even if there should occur any of the rolls called *azar* or *reazar* while they are waiting for one of those totals they both took for themselves: no one shall win by it, nor shall anyone lose, until it is decided by those specific luck-rolls [the points], just as I said above.

### This is the game of marlota.

There is another game of dice they call <term>marlota</term> <note>The name is likely derived from the Arabic *mallūta*. Unlike other games of the period, it focuses on "points" rather than automatic wins.</note> in which there is no <term>azar</term>, nor <term>re-azar</term>, nor <term>iuga</term> <note>These were technical terms for automatic winning or losing rolls in the game of Azar; their absence here makes Marlota a game of assigned targets.</note>.

And it is played by "divided luck" in this manner: They throw the dice, and the caster must give a <term>suerte</term> <note>original: *suerte*; here meaning a target point or assigned number.</note> to the opponent with whom he plays. And the points that they can have or take for themselves in this game are these: seven, eight, nine, ten, eleven, twelve, thirteen, or fourteen, in whatever manner

<column-break/>

they may land, just as we said in the other games regarding how they can land on the top face. Or, seven is a number that is not a point for one or the other before the pairing <note>meaning before the two players have their respective target numbers assigned</note>, so they must play as many times as necessary until a point is established. These [points] are given to the one with whom he plays, and he takes another for himself. With these points, he must strike his own first, and then the other player will play for theirs. And after the points are divided in this way, he must continue throwing until his point comes up or the other person's does; and in this way, by hitting his own he wins, and by throwing the other's he loses.

**This is the game of Ciffa.** <note>original: *Ciffa*; the name is likely derived from the Arabic *sifr*, meaning "zero" or "cipher," though here it designates a specific game of chance.</note>
<column-break/>
**this same way, throws more points, he wins. If he throws the same number, it is a draw <note>original: *manna*; a technical term used in the 13th century to indicate a tie or a "draw" in a game.</note>. If he throws fewer, he loses.**

There is another kind of game called <term>ciffa</term> which is played in this manner: The one who first throws the dice must cast them as many times as is necessary until he throws a pair <note>original: *par*; two dice showing the same value.</note> on two of them. After that, he must throw the other <note>referring to the third die.</note>. Then, the points of this third die must be counted together with the points of the other two that were thrown first. And if the other person who plays against him, by throwing the dice in
<column-break/>

# OF THE DICE

<note>A detailed manuscript illumination set within a dark grey rectangular frame. The scene features six figures gathered around a square wooden gaming board placed on the ground, on which three white dice are visible. On the left side, three men are kneeling; the central figure in a greyish-blue tunic is actively reaching toward the board to roll the dice. ### This game they call half-hazard:

There is another manner of dice game called <term>half-hazard</term> <note>original: *medio azar*; a variation of Hazard, a popular medieval gambling game</note> which is played in this way. Those who wish [to play must decide] in which place they are to throw; first, let them count the spots <note>roll the dice to see who gets the highest number</note>, and the one who wins shall throw first. And if he throws eight points, or six, or seven, or ten and below—in whatever manner these <term>hazards</term> <note>original: *azar*; certain totals that resulted in an immediate win or loss depending on the specific rules</note> may come—they shall be strong. And for every hazard, he shall take one stake from what they have wagered among themselves,

<column-break/>

whether the stake be worth a <term>dinero</term> <note>A small silver coin.</note>, or a <term>sueldo</term> <note>A unit of currency worth twelve dineros.</note>, or a <term>maravedí</term> <note>A gold or silver coin of significant value.</note>, or as high as the <term>wager</term> <note>original: *postura*.</note> may be. And the <term>lucks</term> <note>original: *suertes*; the target totals a player must roll to win if they don't hit a hazard immediately.</note> that are in the middle of these are called "strong [lucks]"; these are eight, nine, ten, eleven, twelve, and thirteen. And if by chance he does not throw a hazard but gives a "luck" to the other player and takes a "luck" for himself, whichever [total] comes first shall win three stakes. And if after he gives a "luck" to the other he then throws a hazard before he takes a "luck" for himself, he shall lose as many stakes. And if he throws two hazards, he shall lose five. And for as many hazards as one throws one after another, each shall be worth one stake.

<summary>This page introduces the rules for "Half-Hazard," a dice game involving specific winning totals (hazards) and target totals (lucks), along with the betting values for various rolls.</summary>

...will ensure that he holds the <term>luck</term> <note>original: *suerte*; in this context, the target number a player aims to hit to win the round.</note> for himself. And so that they may determine who goes first before they begin to play against each other’s luck, they throw one die; whoever rolls a <term>hazard</term> <note>original: *azar*; a specific winning or losing roll according to the rules of the game.</note> wins a point for as many stakes as were wagered. And he shall not surrender the "luck" to the others until he has won that many points over them, and in this manner the game is resumed as from the beginning.

### This game they call raised hazard.

**T**he hazard that they call **raised** <note>original: *pujado*; meaning "bid up" or "increased," referring to the escalating stakes.</note> is played in this manner: it follows the rules of the half-hazard that we described above, except that they always raise the points <column-break/> both by a hazard and by any other count that results in the luck of one player or the other. And for this reason, they call this "raised hazard," because the defense <note>original: *defesa*; likely referring to the mechanics of countering an opponent's roll or stake.</note> of it is [based on] half-hazard.

This is the game they call <term>guirguiesca</term> <note>A specific variation of a dice game, likely related to the broader family of Hazard games popular in the Middle Ages.</note>. There is a type of game called guirguiesca which is played with three dice in this manner. ¶ Those who wish to play must first throw for the <term>entry</term> <note>original: *intraia*; the initial roll to determine who takes the first turn or "enters" the game.</note>. ¶ And he who wins it shall throw first. might come, none of these shall be called <term>luck</term> <note>original: *suerte*; the target number or "point" that a player must try to roll again to win.</note>. And he shall play for whatever the stake may be, if he has agreed to go for it or if he remains silent <note>In medieval gaming law, silence often signified consent to the current stakes.</note>. ¶ And if by chance he does not throw an encounter and instead throws one of the target numbers that we said above were "hazards," he shall lose it all. ¶ And if he throws neither an encounter nor a hazard, but throws one of the other target numbers, he shall take that one for himself. He shall then throw in turns until his own number comes up or the other player's comes up. And by throwing his own, he wins...

-># BOOK OF THE TABLES<-

Here begins the book of the games of
the <term>tables</term> <note>original: *tablas*; a family of board games, including the ancestor of modern backgammon, played with pieces moved according to dice rolls.</note>. And it speaks first of
how the board should be made, and the
pieces, and how many there should be: and what is the
nature and the manner of them. And also of
how they have need of the dice to guide
themselves by them, and make their plays as
principally as they can.
Since we have 
spoken here of the
dice as 
completely as
we could: we
wish now here
to speak of the
tables. For although
they require dice with which they
are played, which show fortune <note>In the medieval context, *ventura* or fortune refers to the element of luck or divine whim.</note>,
because they must be played 
entirely by taking from the wit <note>original: *seso*; meaning the intellect, strategy, or wisdom of the player.</note> there

—now speak of them here. ¶ And
we say this: that the board on which
they are to play must be square, and in the
middle there must be a mark in such a way
that four quadrants <note>original: *quadras*; the four sections of a backgammon board.</note> are made, and in each 
quadrant there must be six <term>houses</term> <note>original: *casas*; the individual points or spaces on the board.</note>, so that
there are twenty-four in all. ¶ And 
although in some lands they make 
the houses of the tables flat and 
painted, ¶ also the pieces with which
they play square or round, and
do not make a place in which they fit: for
this reason it was found better to make a
frame <note>original: *fuste*.</note> for the board in the manner 
of a half-wheel <note>original: *media rueda*; this refers to the characteristic semi-circular or triangular grooves carved into the wood to hold the circular pieces in place.</note>, in which the 
pieces, which are to be round, can fit.
¶ Likewise, it is necessary that half of the

of the half of the board, they can be—

<keywords>Alfonso X, Book of Games, Tables, backgammon, game board construction, strategy vs luck, medieval games, game pieces</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Book of Tables," specifically explaining the strategic necessity of doubling pieces on a single point to prevent capture, and beginning the technical explanation of how to count movements on the board based on dice rolls.</meta>

...to have in each point <term>two</term> <note>original: *dos*; the text refers to the practice of "making a point" or stacking two pieces to prevent the opponent from landing there.</note> or at least a pair on the outside, to maneuver the game or to direct it as much as might be necessary. 

¶ For without these [doubled pieces] the game could not be played. ¶ And for this reason, they place the <term>tables</term> <note>original: *tablas*; in this context, the word refers to the individual game pieces, often called "men" or "stones" in modern backgammon.</note> doubled; because just as in the game of chess, when one finds a piece alone and separated from the others with no one to guard it, the opponent can capture it. 

Likewise in the game of tables: if the pieces are not doubled, another player who rolls the number corresponding to that point can capture that lone piece, and there is no one to defend it. 

¶ The <term>maneuver</term> <note>original: *barata*; refers to strategic movement, positioning, or the "tricks" of the game used to trap an opponent.</note> of the tables occurs when one player captures so many of the other’s pieces that the opponent has no points left where they may re-enter the board, and thus loses the game. 

¶ And the rule is that even if he has more pieces and can enter with them, he cannot play, regardless of whether either player wishes to. 

¶ Therefore, due to both this maneuvering and the reduction of pieces (if there are more than thirteen), the game could not proceed.

**¶ Concerning those things which the tables must have by necessity.**

For just as the body could not move without feet, so the pieces would not move without [the board] to play any game; and by direct <term>necessity</term> <note>original: *fuerça*; here meaning the binding rules of movement dictated by the dice.</note> according to the points of the dice, they must move into those points that are designated for them. 

¶ And the designation is as follows: in the points of the inner quarters of the board, the points of the dice are arranged in this manner. 

¶ In the first point of the board's division, near the outer edge where there is no other point: it is for the six. The next one beside it is for the five, the next for four, the next for three, the next for two, and the last for the ace. 

¶ Therefore, whoever wishes to count from that point near the <term>bar</term> <note>original: *barra*; the middle dividing line of the game board.</note> where there are no points until the next quarter

<column-break/>

of the board that is beside it, the six would be at the first point of the adjacent quarter. 

¶ And this is because the point where the piece is currently located is never counted; for if it were counted, the piece would remain in the same spot. 

Therefore, for a roll of six or any other, one must count starting from the very next point ahead of it. 

¶ In this way, they make all the pieces move across the board. And the games are played according to the rules you will see in this book. 

¶ Thus, it is necessary for all who wish to learn them well to first know how many points each space represents, not counting the space from which the piece starts.

# THE BOOK OF TABLES

<note>A rectangular miniature painting framed in red and gold borders. The scene depicts two men seated on cushions against a deep blue background, playing a variant of the game of tables, which is an early ancestor of backgammon. The figure on the left wears a hooded grey cloak over a red tunic and red hose with yellow shoes; he gestures toward the board as if explaining a move. The figure on the right, with shoulder-length blonde hair, wears a green tunic over a patterned red-and-white garment. Between them is a tall wooden game board containing two columns of twelve round playing pieces—known as "tables"—with yellow pieces on the left and red on the right. Three white dice are visible in the center of the board. Below the frame of the miniature, a decorative cord with a white tassel hangs from the board's stand.</note>

## This game they call "The Twelve Dogs," or "The Twelve Brothers."

The second <term>game</term> <note>original: *uuego*; here referring to a specific variant of the rules.</note> is one in which the <term>points</term> <note>original: *casas* (literally "houses"); the twenty-four triangular spaces on a backgammon board.</note> are doubled, and it is called "Twelve Brothers" or "Twelve Dogs." This is because it is played with twelve <term>pieces</term> <note>original: *tablas*; the flat, circular markers used in the game.</note>, so that they all end up "doubled" <note>In this context, "doubled" (*dobladas*) means stacking two pieces on a single point, which in many medieval games made them safe from capture.</note> in the six points of the inner quadrant of the board, whichever one the player who goes first chooses.

And whichever of the two players can double them [stack them] the soonest shall win, because the opponent cannot capture a piece as long as the point is <gloss>occupied by a</gloss> doubled <gloss>stack</gloss>. However, each of those who play must keep the pieces

<column-break/>

in their hand, or on the board outside of the points in which they are to play, and not place them into the points until the luck of the <term>dice</term> <note>original: *dados*.</note> comes, according to which they must enter them.

And this is the explanation of this game.

<summary>This page describes "The Twelve Dogs" (or "Twelve Brothers"), a game variant played with twelve pieces where players compete to be the first to stack their pieces in a single quadrant.</summary>
<keywords>Twelve Dogs, Twelve Brothers, stacking pieces, medieval backgammon, Alfonso X, game rules, dice games</keywords>

# TABLES

<note>A framed rectangular miniature painting with a red, black, and blue border. Set against a deep blue background, four figures gather around a central game board. On the left, a woman in a pink mantle and dark green dress stands behind a young boy seated on a wooden stool; the boy reaches toward the board. ->This game they call Doublet.<-

There is this other game of <term>tables</term> <note>original: *tablas*; the general name for games played on a board with dice and markers, like backgammon.</note> that they call Doublet, which is played in this manner: Each of the players must have twelve <term>pieces</term> <note>original: *tablas*; the flat, circular markers.</note> and place them <term>doubled</term> <note>original: *dobladas*; stacked one on top of the other.</note>, one upon the other, each in their quadrant of the board, so that they are directly across from one another. And whoever wins the <term>opening toss</term> <note>original: *iunta*; the initial roll to determine the first move.</note> shall cast first. And those twelve pieces that are on top of the others must be <term>lowered</term> <note>original: *laxar*; to unstack the top piece and place it on the board space according to the dice roll.</note> according to the luck of the points on the dice. And likewise, they must then be <term>borne off</term> <note>original: *leuar*; to remove pieces from the board entirely.</note>; and whoever bears them off first will win the game. And if by ch—

<column-break/>

—ance either of the players should cast a roll for which he has no <term>legal moves</term> <note>original: *mulas*; literally "mules." In this context, it refers to dice rolls that cannot be used by the player who threw them.</note> to make—whether to lower the pieces or to bear them off—the other player must do it instead. And in this way it often happens that one player wins by the rolls cast by the other. And this is the explanation of this game, and this is the illustration of the <term>setup</term> <note>original: *entablamiento*; the starting configuration of the pieces.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the rules and setup for "Doublet," a game of tables where pieces start stacked in pairs. A unique feature of this variant is that if a player cannot use their dice roll, their opponent is permitted to use it for their own benefit.</summary>

# BOOK OF GAMES

<note>A rectangular manuscript illumination depicts two men playing a board game.
