**This is the game that they call Failures.**

There is another game of <term>tables</term> <note>original: *tablas*; a category of board games played with dice and pieces, the ancestors of modern backgammon.</note> like this one that they call <term>Failures</term> <note>original: *fallas*; likely named for the "failings" or "faults" that occur when a player cannot make a legal move.</note>. It is set up in this manner: thirteen of the fifteen <term>pieces</term> <note>original: *tablas*; the circular markers used by the players.</note> are placed in the "house of the six" <note>the sixth point or space on the board.</note> which is on the inside in one <term>quadrant</term> <note>original: *quadra*; one of the four sections of the game board.</note> of the board; and the [other] three <note>While the text says fifteen pieces total, it specifies placing thirteen and then mentions "the three" (tres); this may be a scribal variation or error for the remaining two pieces.</note> are placed in the house that is opposite that one, on the outside in the other quadrant of the board that is directly across from it. ¶ And he who occupies that quadrant sets up his pieces in this same manner as the other player we have described. ¶ And when they are thus set up, each of the players must strive to bring his pieces directly toward his own quadrant as quickly as he can, for there he must arrange them in such a way that he has them in safety. In

<column-break/>

bearing them off <note>original: *leuando*; the process of removing pieces from the board after they have all reached the final quadrant.</note>, he must take care that the other player does not "hit" them, for if he did not have an open "house" <note>an unoccupied or protected space.</note> to move to, he would have to return to the quadrant from which he first moved them. ¶ And if he should find that where he intended to enter, the other player had already established his own pieces, he would find that he could not enter, and he would lose the game because of it. ¶ Furthermore, he who throws the dice in such a way that his move has no place to land except in a house that the other player has already filled would lose the game because he "failed" <note>original: *fallecie*; he defaulted or failed to make a legal move.</note>. ¶ Wherefore, for this reason, they call this game "Failures." ¶ But the game can also be lost or won in another way: if by chance neither of the players brings his pieces safely to his own quadrant so that they do not strike one another, the one who bears them off the fastest will win the game. ¶ This is the <term>arrangement</term> <note>original: *rrepartimiento*; the layout and specific rules of play.</note> of this game.

<summary>This page describes the rules and setup for "Fallas" (Failures), a backgammon-style game where players lose automatically if their dice rolls force them into spaces occupied by their opponent.</summary>

# TABLES

<note>A colored miniature painting depicts two men seated on the ground playing a board game. *This is the game that they call the six, two, and ace.*

There is another game they call the six, two, and ace. Both players set up their pieces in one <term>quadrant</term> <note>original: *quadra*; one of the four sections of the "tables" or backgammon board.</note> of the board. The one who wins the <term>opening roll</term> <note>original: *medida*; literally "the measure," determining who takes the lead.</note> must place the fifteen <term>pieces</term> <note>original: *tablas*; the circular markers used by players.</note> in this manner: in the "house of the six" <note>the sixth point or space in the quadrant.</note> on the inside of the board in that quadrant, eight pieces; in the house of the three in this same quadrant, four pieces; and in that of the <term>ace</term> <note>original: *as*; the first point or a roll of one.</note>, three pieces. And the other player must place his pieces in this same quadrant: in the houses of the five, the four, and the two, five pieces in each one. Because the one who wins the lead [takes] the houses of the six, two, and ace, they call this game by that name. <note>The text notes the name "Six, Two, and Ace," though the first player's setup actually occupies the 6, 3, and 1. This suggests the name might refer to the specific configuration of the board or a variation in regional naming.</note> He must play first and take as many houses as

<column-break/>

he can in the other quadrant that is in front of him. And the other, who plays after him, shall likewise take as many houses as he can in this same quadrant. And if, in taking the houses, one player <term>hits</term> <note>original: *ficiere... una tavla*; to land on a space occupied by a single opposing piece, common in the backgammon family.</note> one or more of the other’s pieces, he must take them not to the quadrant directly across, nor to the house facing it, but to the one that is <term>diagonal</term> <note>original: *en pespunta*; literally "backstitch," implying a diagonal or non-linear movement to the opposite side.</note>. And after he puts them in that quadrant, he must bring them <term>backwards</term> <note>original: *en rretros*; moving pieces through the sections of the board.</note> through all the quadrants until reaching the quadrant where they must be <term>borne off</term> <note>original: *levar*; the final stage of the game where pieces are removed from the board.</note>. And whoever bears them off the fastest shall win the game. And this is the explanation of this game; if it should end, we would say so.

<summary>This page describes the setup and rules for a backgammon-style game called "Six, Two, and Ace," characterized by its specific starting positions and a unique "backstitch" movement for captured pieces.</summary>

<note>A rectangular miniature with a multi-colored border of red, blue, and white depicts two noblewomen. ->This game they call in Spain the Emperor: because he created it.<-

There is another game of tables that they call in Spain the "Emperor" because he created it: it is played with <term>pieces</term> <note>original: *tablas*; the circular markers used by the players.</note> and is played in this way. One player must place the fifteen pieces in the <term>house of the ace</term> <note>the first point or space in a section of the board.</note> in one <term>quadrant</term> <note>original: *quadra*; one of the four six-point sections of the "tables" board.</note>. And in the other [section], the other fifteen pieces [are placed] in the other house of the ace of the quadrant that is in that same relative position. And the one who wins the <term>opening roll</term> <note>original: *baralla*; the initial contest or throw to determine who takes the lead.</note> has to throw first and move the pieces according to the luck of the dice through the quadrants of the board, until he <term>establishes</term> <note>original: *etable*; to bring the pieces into the final "home" quadrant.</note> them in the quadrant where the other player has his. And this same thing the other player must do against him. While they are moving to join them together, if one piece hits another, that piece must return to

<column-break/>

the quadrant where it was first placed. And so they must bring them out once again as they did at first until they reach the quadrant where they should be established. And by joining more [pieces] against others and hitting them and returning them again as before, the player who manages to establish them in that quadrant where they belong and carries them to safety wins the game. And there are two things that good players do. One is the <term>manerilla</term> <note>original: *manerilla*; a defensive "little maneuver" or blockade.</note> where he will join them. The <term>manerilla</term> is done to defend oneself so that the one with the worse game does not lose it. The <term>iuntara</term> <note>original: *iuntara*; a strategy of "joining" pieces together to secure a lead.</note> is to win the game more safely for the player who has the better position. The manner [of the strategy] is in this way: that by joining together a large quantity of pieces so that they [the opponent] cannot enter the houses of the other's quadrant by a strong <term>throw</term> <note>original: *alance*; the casting of the dice.</note>, he <term>blocks</term> <note>original: *descerra*; literally to "unlock" or "close off," here meaning to control the spaces.</note> all the houses and points with his pieces and those of the other. And they must take care while one piece is made most secure in the houses of the

# BOOK OF THE [TABLES]

<note>A rectangular illumination at the top of the page depicts two men seated on opposite sides of a large, upright wooden board used for a game of tables, a family of games that includes modern backgammon. <margin>This game they call the entrance pair</margin>

There is another game they call the "entrance pair" <note>original: *la pareia de entrada*</note> which is played with three dice or with two in this manner. ¶ The players who play this game must have the pieces <note>original: *tablas*; the circular "men" or stones used in the game</note> outside the "houses" <note>the quadrants of the board</note>. They must enter them into one of the four quadrants of the board as they agree, just like in the game of "Twelve Dogs" <note>original: *doze canes*; a simpler tables variant where pieces are entered onto the board based on dice rolls</note>. And he who first enters them there must bring them through the quadrants of the board until they reach the other quadrant that is directly across from it. There they "entable" them <note>original: *entablam*; to arrange pieces on the points of the board</note> if they are able to fill the points. ¶ And if the pieces enter their path toward the quadrant where they must be "borne off" <note>original: *leuar*; to remove pieces from the board to win the game</note>, if they find any piece exposed, they must return it to that quadrant where they were first ar-

<column-break/>

-ranged. ¶ And he must strive to enter it as much as he can into that quadrant from which they must be borne off. And whoever bears them off the fastest shall win the game. ¶ And a player gains a great advantage whenever they throw a pair <note>doubles</note> with the three dice or with the two, for they must move according to that roll in which they threw the pair. ¶ And furthermore, they shall have the "hand" <note>the right or turn</note> to throw the dice again. ¶ And because at the beginning of this game they enter with the pieces just as in "Twelve Dogs," and afterwards when they throw a pair they must complete the rest of the movement, for this reason they call this game the "entrance pair."

# TABLES

<note>An illuminated miniature within a green border depicts two men seated across from each other, playing a board game on a large wooden table with hinges, resembling a backgammon board. ->This game they call Cab y quinal:<-

There is another game of tables <note>original: *tablas*; the family of games including modern backgammon</note> that they call *Cab y quinal* <note>The name likely refers to the starting positions on the points for six (often called *cab* or *cap* in dice games) and five (*quinal*)</note>. ¶ This game is played with all thirty pieces <note>original: *tablas*; the circular "men" or stones</note>. They are placed in one quarter of the four quadrants that make up the board. Fifteen pieces of one color are placed in the "house" <note>the individual point or triangular space</note> of the six, and the other fifteen in the house of the five, which is located next to it. ¶ The pieces on the five-point have the lead <note>original: *mano*; the first move</note>. This is because if those on the six-point—which are one point further ahead—were to play first, they would have two advantages: the one point of distance they already have, and the advantage of the first move. ¶ The game is played in this way: the pieces must be moved around through the other houses of the quadrants of the board until they reach the quadrant that is directly across from where they were first placed; and from there they must be borne off <note>original: *leuar*; to remove pieces from the board after they have reached the final quadrant</note>. ¶ And

<column-break/>

if any of them are "hit" in passing <note>original: *trauesso*; similar to being "hit" in backgammon when a piece is left alone on a point</note>, the player must take them and re-enter with them into the houses of the quadrant from which they first moved. They may enter if they find the points empty, or occupied by a single piece <note>original: *senzillas*</note>, or on their own "safe spots" <note>original: *fugas*</note>, whichever they may be. ¶ And in this manner is the game of *Cab y quinal* played with three dice. ¶ But if any should wish to play it with two dice: they take the other roll from whatever luck they chance upon, and they must place the pieces as if the roll had been a six. To set the board <note>original: *en tablarse*</note> in this way, they are to put fourteen pieces in the house of the six, and one of these same pieces further ahead in the house of the six <note>This likely means the first point of the adjacent quadrant</note>. ¶ And of the other fifteen pieces, they must put fourteen in the house of the five, and one in the house of the other point that is next to it. And the movement and the bearing off of pieces from these houses is to be done just like the other game we described, which we said is played with three dice.

# BOOK OF THE <note>original: *Libro de las [Tablas]*; this is the heading for the section dedicated to board games played with pieces and dice, ancestors of modern backgammon.</note>

<note>A large horizontal illumination is divided into two square panels. In the left panel, two men sit on a red and yellow vertically striped bench playing a board game. The man on the left wears a light pink tunic and a white coif, while the man on the right wears a green tunic and a red cap. ->This game they call "All Tables."<-

There is another game they call "All Tables" <note>original: *todas tablas*</note> because it is set up with a fitting arrangement <note>original: *uiña auiniente*; an archaic term likely referring to the orderly or "convenient" placement of the pieces</note> in all four corners <note>original: *quinas*; the four quadrants of the board</note> of the board.

And in the two quadrants that are directly opposite one another: they place five pieces <note>original: *tablas*; the circular playing stones</note> of one color on the six-point; and they place pieces of this same color on the ace-point <note>the "one" point on the board</note> in the other quadrant that is directly opposite it. In the same way, they also place the pieces in the opposing quadrant as we have described.

And in the other quadrants that are adjacent to these, in the house of the ace, they place five pieces of the same color as the other five they placed in the house of the six.

And on the five-points, they place three pieces of each of those colors.

And they play

<column-break/>

in this way: whoever has the lead <note>original: *la mano*; the first move</note> shall play toward whichever side he wishes, moving the pieces from the ace-point toward the house of the six where the five pieces are kept.

And if any pieces are taken <note>original: *tomaren*; "hit" or captured as in modern backgammon, requiring them to be re-entered</note>, they must be recovered in the quadrant where the two pieces are on the ace-point.

And from there, they are carried to the quadrant where the five pieces are in the house of the six, and from there they are borne off <note>original: *leuarlas*; to remove the pieces from the board once they reach the final quadrant</note>.

**This game is played with three dice:**

<summary>This page introduces "Todas tablas" (All Tables), a complex variant of backgammon featuring a symmetrical starting layout across all four quadrants. It details the specific placement of pieces on the ace, five, and six points and specifies that three dice are used.</summary>

where the <insert>first player's</insert> other fourteen are,
on the six-point; three in the points
of the five, the four, and the three: three pieces

The fifteenth <insert>piece</insert> in the third quadrant
on the ace-point: and so it is set up.
¶ And in this game the pieces
do not hit one another <note>meaning pieces cannot be "captured" or knocked off the board as in modern backgammon</note>. And the move

<column-break/>

that one player is not able

they must be set up will win the
game. ¶ This game has been
found newly <note>discovered or invented recently at the time of the manuscript's writing</note>: and the name does not
accord with the other
ancient ones. :-

## This game the Romans call "Six-Against."

This is another game that the Romans call <term>Six-Against</term> <note>original: *seste enconttra* or *se enconttr*; a backgammon-style game where players’ pieces "encounter" each other while moving in the same direction or competing for space</note>. It is played with three dice. It is played by the throw <note>original: *a lanca*; possibly referring to the initial toss to determine play</note> if one enters first. And he who wins the <term>opening contest</term> <note>original: *baratta*; a preliminary roll or dispute to decide who begins the game</note> plays first. And the pieces of this game are placed <term>entered</term> <note>original: *enttabladas*; the process of placing pieces into the starting positions or "points" on the board</note> in the quadrant of the board to the right hand of the one who plays with them, on the other side of the board. ¶ The white pieces are entered opposite the others of the player who is to play with them. ¶ Each of the players must have fifteen pieces to move through the <term>quadrants</term> <note>original: *quadras*; the four sections of a backgammon-style board</note> of the board until they place them in the quadrant of the board on the opposite side, directly across from where they were first entered. And if they

<column-break/>

encounter and strike one another, the piece that is struck must return to the quadrant where it was first entered. ¶ In this game, one cannot <term>double</term> <note>original: *doblar*; placing two pieces of the same color on a single point to "make" or secure it so the opponent cannot land there</note> any piece until they have passed the halfway point of the board, just as in the <term>Game of the Emperor</term> <note>original: *iuego dell emperador*; a popular medieval backgammon variant previously described in the manuscript</note>. ¶ The <term>special skill</term> <note>original: *fazaña*; literally a "feat" or "deed," here referring to the strategic mastery required</note> of this game is knowing how to double the pieces well after the piece has passed the halfway point of the board, according to the luck of the dice. ¶ This is the explanation of this game. ¶ This is the illustration of the setup.

<summary>This page describes "Seste Encontra" (Six-Against), a Roman variation of the Tables family played with three dice. It details the movement of fifteen pieces across the board and the specific rule that pieces cannot be protected by doubling until they reach the second half of the board.</summary>

# THE GYRFALCON

### Here begins the game of the gyrfalcon
### which was created in India in the likeness
### of how the ancient Kings used to lead
### their armies of horses <note>cavalry</note> and pawns <note>infantry</note> and for
### the others in chess to demonstrate the power
### that Kings have over their enemies.
### And furthermore, how they displayed while
### in the armies strange birds and
### beasts so that other men would obey
### them more willingly and recognize
### them as much more noble.

->Amen<-

**T**his [game],
just like
the other common
chess,
is divided
into twelve
squares <note>meaning a 12x12 board</note>
along the side,
making twelve
ranks. And
just as in
the other chess they placed sixteen
pieces <note>original: "uuegos", literally "games" or "plays", but referring here to the pieces</note> on each side, making thirty-two,
they placed in this one twenty-four
pieces on each side, making forty-
eight. ¶ And just as those
sixteen pieces are reduced by the luck
of the dice into six <note>referring to variants where moves are determined by a six-sided die</note>, likewise in
this game, the twenty-four are reduced to eight.
¶ In this game there is a King who is like
the standard-bearer <note>original: <term>alfferez</term></note> and lord of the entire army
and he moves to the next square like the
<term>alfferza</term> <note>the 'General' or 'Queen' piece in medieval chess, which moved only one square diagonally</note>
or to the first square diagonally <note>original: <term>sosquino</term></note> or
straight as he wishes; and he may capture even
if there is some distance in between.
¶ And there is another piece representing a
bird that is superior over all other birds;
although this bird has many names
according to the languages of
the people, specifically in India
where this game was first created,

<column-break/>

it is named <term>Aanca</term> <note>original: "Aanca", referring to the mythical Anqa or Roc</note>, which means
a very beautiful and
fearsome bird, according to what the
sages record in their books. When this bird
is present, no other bird dares to
take flight, and those that are in the
trees and in the caves do not dare to
leave them, but rather strive to hide
themselves as much as they can. ¶ For
it is so large that it carries the
elephant to its nest, along with all the other
large beasts it finds. And this bird
is very beautiful, for its breast and
throat shine as much as if
they were gilded; its back and wings
are green; and its feet and the eyes of its
beak are red as scarlet; and
it has very black talons. And it has on its
head a round crown of feathers
like a diadem. ¶ This
bird stays always on the highest
crags it finds. And it does so for two
reasons: the first, because it always
desires clear and clean air. And the other,
because it has small legs and
very long wings, and it cannot
take flight from level ground. And whenever
it wishes to move to fly, it lightens itself as
one who wants to jump, and then goes straight
to the part where it wishes to go. And
in this manner, they ordered its move here:
after it leaps like the <term>alfferza</term>
diagonally to one square, it goes
afterward in a straight line as far as it wishes to go.
And in capturing, it removes the piece from the board,
or continues until it finds something to take. ¶ And
the rule of how it leaps is
this: if it is on a black
square, it goes to the first black square
diagonally like the <term>alfferza</term> <note>meaning it skips the immediate neighbor to land on the next square of the same color</note>, and in this
path it moves straight as far as it wishes. Likewise,
if it were on a white square and
departing from the four white squares...

There is nothing it eats on dry land that is not [moistened]. No Bishop, however strong it may be, [is like it]; and when it wants to take something, it makes a pretense of entering another part to secure it; and then it takes it suddenly diagonally <note>original: <term>sosquino</term></note> and goes after it until it takes it. And the horn of the forehead is sharp and cuts very strongly; and this unicorn has a body as large as an elephant and the color of ash, and legs and feet like the elephant. **T**he rhinoceros, when it moves and when it is enraged, its eyes turn red like rubies; and it runs very fast as is fitting, and first makes a leap sideways like a horse <note>the knight's move</note>. And so they established it in this chess, that it moves the first leap like a horse, and afterwards diagonally <note>original: <term>sosquino</term></note> like the <term>alferza</term> <note>the medieval predecessor to the Queen, which moved only one square diagonally</note> as far as it wishes, or until it captures. In that square where they leap, they cannot take others except always forward. **T**he Lion is also a very strong beast and leaps much sideways or straight, more than any other beast when it wants to take something. For this likeness they placed it here, and it leaps to the fourth square—one [step] straight and two sideways <note>this describes a "long" knight move, specifically a (3,1) leaper</note>. **T**he Rook <note>original: <term>roque</term></note> is in the likeness of the wings <note>flanks</note> of the knights, and they play there like the [standard piece] of the other chess. **T**he Pawns <note>original: <term>peones</term></note> are made in the likeness of the common people, and they play in this manner as we have said above. **A**nd when the pawn reaches the end <note>the promotion rank</note> in this game of chess, it becomes like the piece that belongs to the square where it reaches. If it reaches the King’s square, it becomes like the Lady <note>original: <term>dama</term>, here used for the promoted Queen piece</note>. **T**he pawns are set up in this chess on the fourth rank from the major pieces. And we wish to show what superiority some pieces have over [others]...

->### CHESS<-

...the others, so that he who plays with them may know them, so that he does not give up a better piece for a weaker one.

**T**he King <note>original: <term>feyto</term>; likely a variant or specific title for the King in this 12x12 version</note> is the greatest and best piece, as we have said above. **A**nd after the King <note>original: <term>fey</term></note>, the Aanca <note>original: <term>aanca</term>; a legendary bird of prey of immense size, similar to a Roc or Phoenix</note> is a better piece than the Unicorn. **A**nd the Unicorn is a better piece than the Rook. **A**nd the Rook is better than the Lion. **A**nd the Lion is better than the Crocodile <note>original: <term>cocatriz</term>; while it sounds like "cockatrice," in this manuscript it refers to the crocodile</note>. **A**nd the Crocodile is better than the Giraffe <note>original: <term>zamfria</term></note>. **A**nd the Giraffe is better than the Pawn.

**A**nd this hierarchy <note>original: <term>meloria</term>; literally "superiority" or "ranking"</note> is shown best by the dice, which are made in the way we shall describe later in this book, for when players wish to use them for this game of Great Chess. And this is the illustration of the board, and of the pieces, and of how they must be set up on it, and their arrangement.

->DICE DICE<-

### Here it speaks of the eight-sided dice and how they are made.

Because <insert>this game of <term>Chess</term> <note>original: "Acedrex"; here referring to "Great Chess," a complex variant played on a 12x12 board</note> is played very slowly and they take a long time over it, therefore we,</insert> Alfonso, ordered dice to be made so that this Chess might be played more quickly, and that the superiority of the <term>pieces</term> <note>original: "trebejos"; a general term for gaming pieces</note> might be shown by the luck of these dice. ¶ The dice are made in this way: they have eight faces, and each face is in the shape of a triangle because it has three edges. In no other way could they be made to belong to this game; for even though the shape is odd, one face must by necessity fall flat on top. <note>The text describes an octahedron. Unlike a cube, the "top" face of an octahedron is parallel to the bottom face, allowing for a clear result.</note> And for this reason, these eight-faced dice were made for the eight ranks of the pieces. ¶ On the first face, there are eight points, and on another, seven, and so they go decreasing until they reach one. ¶ Because the King is the greatest and best, he has the 8 points. ¶ The <term>Adarca</term> <note>original: "adarca"; a piece in Great Chess, likely named after a leather shield</note> has seven points. ¶ The Unicorn has six. ¶ The <term>Rook</term> <note>original: "roque"</note> has five. ¶ The Lion has four points. ¶ The Knight has three. ¶ The <term>Bishop</term> <note>original: "alffil"; derived from the Arabic "al-fil" (the elephant)</note> has two. ¶ The Pawns have one point. As we 

<column-break/>

said below regarding the hierarchy of the pieces. ¶ And with these eight-faced dice, they can play as many games as with the other six-sided ones <note>standard cubic dice</note> that we have told of previously in this book. And it is shown in this way: just as in the other six-sided dice that we use, the highest points are eighteen, seventeen, sixteen, and fifteen, and these are <term>Azar</term> <note>original: "azar"; from the Arabic "al-zahr," meaning a specific winning or losing throw in a dice game of the same name</note>. And the counterparts of these lucks are six, five, four, and three, and these are also "azar." And they take the luck from fifteen points upward and from six downward. Likewise, in these eight-faced dice, "azar" are twenty-points, and twenty-one, and twenty-two, and twenty-three, and twenty-four. And the counterparts of these points are also "azar," which are seven points, six, five, four, and three. And from twenty points upward and from seven downward, they may take their luck. And from here on, they may play whatever game they wish with these dice. ¶ And this is the division of these dice, and this is their illustration that is painted here.

<summary>King Alfonso X describes the design and purpose of eight-sided dice (octahedrons), which were created to speed up the play of Great Chess by assigning point values to each piece rank. The text also explains how to adapt the rules of the popular dice game 'Azar' for use with these eight-sided dice.</summary>
<keywords>Alfonso X, Great Chess, dice, octahedron, gaming pieces, Azar, probability, medieval games</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the "Great Chess" or "Decimal Chess" played on a 10x10 board, moving from the movement of the pieces to the construction of the specialized dice used to play this variant.</meta>

->AND CONCERNING THE DICE<-

## Here is described how the dice for this chess of ten squares are made, and how many points are on them.

Because this chess requires a greater number of points to be played more quickly, we ordered dice to be made to play it. And also, so that through the results of the rolls <note>Spanish: *suertes*, referring to the luck or outcome of the dice</note>, the mastery of the games may be demonstrated. These dice are made in this manner: they have seven faces <note>Spanish: *llanas*, literally 'flats'</note>, and the highest roll on them is seven points, for otherwise they could not be made in a way that belongs to this specific chess. And because the shape of these dice is odd-numbered, they must necessarily have a sharp ridge <note>Spanish: *refluso agudo*</note> and five rolls fall on the die's edges. The roll that falls facing the person who throws them is his. And the other edges <note>Spanish: *aguijondas*</note> were designed so that the rolls cannot be mistaken; whatever roll faces the thrower must be cast until it can be clearly determined.

<note>A circular diagram outlined in blue. It is divided horizontally by a red line. The top half is blue and contains small white characters or symbols; the bottom half is red.</note>

Just as in the other greater chess where the twelve games are determined by dice with eight points, so in this chess of ten, the games are determined by these dice with seven points. On the first face of these dice there are seven points, and on the next six, and so they go descending and decreasing the points in this manner until there is no more than one. And because the King is the greatest and best piece <note>Spanish: *iuego*, here referring to the chess piece itself</note>, he has the seven points. And the Counselor <note>Spanish: *alfferza*, the precursor to the modern Queen</note> has the six. And the

<column-break/>

Rook <note>Spanish: *roque*</note> has the five. And the Knight <note>Spanish: *cauallo*</note> has the four. And the Judge <note>term: Iuyz|A specialized piece in Alfonso's decimal chess that moves differently than the standard pieces.</note> has the three. And the <term>Alfil</term> <note>The ancestor of the modern Bishop, which at this time moved in short leaps.</note> has the two. And the Pawn <note>Spanish: *peon*</note> has the one. And this is the distribution of these dice. And this is their shape.

<note>A blank space intended for an illustration, showing only very faint, ghostly outlines of a circular or geometric diagram, possibly representing another die face.</note>

And likewise, there are other dice of these same values that we mentioned regarding the ridge; they have as many points as these, and this is the distribution found on these other faces. They have two flat faces; if they fall on the flat ridge, it is seven points, and on the other ridge six points. And these flat faces have five edges, and if these dice fall on the other five values that remain, they can only come up sharp like a spine on top. And in these five values, the distribution is the same as the other dice we mentioned above. And this is their figure and how they are made.

<summary>This page describes the design and numbering of specialized seven-sided dice used for a 10x10 version of chess. It assigns specific point values to each piece, from the King (7) down to the Pawn (1), to dictate play.</summary>
<keywords>dice, decimal chess, game pieces, King, Counselor, Rook, Knight, Judge, Alfil, Pawn, seven-sided dice, Alfonso X</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the variants of "Great Chess" (played on a 10x10 board) to the corresponding versions of "Tables" (a family of games including Backgammon) played on similar expanded boards.</meta>

->DOUBLE TABLES<-

### Here begins the game of tables, or chess of the ten houses; it is played with the dice of seven faces.

Since we have shown you the other games of <term>chess of the ten houses</term> <note>original: *acedrex delas diez casas*; this refers to "Great Chess" played on a 10x10 board</note>, explaining how they are made and how they can be played as we described above, we now wish to show you the <term>game of tables</term> <note>original: *iuego delas tablas*; "Tables" is the medieval Spanish term for games played with pieces and dice on a board, similar to modern Backgammon</note> that belongs to these others. The board must be square, with paths between the rows like the other boards, and with "houses" <note>the points or spaces on the board</note> in which the pieces are set up, as we have already said. And in each quadrant there must be seven houses, where the highest roll of the dice <note>the dice used for this version have seven sides</note> is the greatest luck; thus, there are twenty-eight houses in total. These pieces must be round, and the houses in which they are set up should be hollowed out in the manner of <note>original: *media nueues*; half-walnut shells</note> so that they may fit better. Also, it is necessary that half of these pieces be of one color and the other half another so they are distinguished from each other; there shall be thirty-four pieces in all: seventeen of one color and seventeen of the other. In one quadrant of the board, they can place three pieces in each house, and three will remain at the end to play the game or to

<column-break/>

capture them <note>original: *mannarle*; to "eat" or capture a piece</note> when necessary in their houses; however, three pieces together cannot be captured. For this reason, they place the pieces "doubled" <note>stacked or protected</note>, because just as in the game of chess, when one finds a single <term>piece</term> <note>original: *treteiro*; a piece or "trifle" of the game</note> belonging to the other that has no one to guard it, he may take it, as we said before. Likewise, in this game of Tables, if the pieces are not doubled as we described, the other player who finds a single piece unprotected will take it, since there is no one to defend it. And the <term>victory</term> <note>original: *barata*; a term for a strategic win or gambit</note> occurs when one player takes so many pieces from the other that the opponent has no houses left in which to enter them, and thus loses the game. And in this game of Tables, they can play as many variants as on the other board played with six-sided dice, as we described above, except that while on six-sided dice the highest roll is six points, in the dice of "Great Chess"—which are used for the points here—the highest roll one can take is <note>original: *dizie siete*; seventeen</note> seventeen points. Thus, by ascending or descending through the other rolls, they can play whatever games they wish, according to the luck of the dice. This is the arrangement of this game, and this is the illustration of the setup and how the "paired" game is played on this board; for this reason, they have placed its image here.

<summary>This page describes "Great Tables," a 28-space variant of Backgammon designed to accompany 10x10 "Great Chess," using 17 pieces per player and seven-sided dice.</summary>
<keywords>Tables, Backgammon, Great Chess, seven-sided dice, board games, Alfonso X, medieval gaming, rules of play</keywords>

->CHESS<-

### Here begins another Chess variant which was made in the likeness of the four seasons of the year, which the ancient sages added:

There is another Chess variant <term>Acerder</term> <note>original: *Acerder*, a variant spelling of *acedrex* (chess)</note> that the ancient sages made in the likeness of the four seasons of the year, and it was organized in this way. ¶ The first season is Spring <note>original: *verano*. In the 13th century, *verano* referred to the "vernal" or spring season, while *estio* referred to the height of summer.</note> and it begins in mid-March and lasts until mid-June. ¶ The second season is Summer and it begins in mid-June and lasts until mid-September. ¶ The third season is Autumn and it begins in mid-September and lasts until mid-December. ¶ The fourth season is Winter and it begins in mid-December and lasts until mid-March. ¶ And these four seasons they divided according to the four elements. ¶ Spring by the element of Air. ¶ Summer by the element of Fire. ¶ Autumn by the element of Earth. ¶ Winter by the element of Water. ¶ And because in the first season of Spring, which we mentioned above, all things grow and the trees and grasses bloom again—because its element is Air, which is clearer than in any of the other seasons—therefore they made this season's color green. ¶ And Summer, which is hotter and drier than the other seasons, they made in the likeness of Fire, which is of this nature; and therefore they made this season's color red, because of its element. ¶ Autumn is dry and cold because it belongs to the element of Earth, and it is more temperate than Summer, leaning more toward coldness than toward heat; the things that remained scorched from the Summer are born and bloom again in this season. And because its element is Earth and its <term>complexion</term> <note>A medical/philosophical term referring to the balance of qualities—hot, cold, moist, dry—within a body or substance.</note> is coldness and dryness, they made this season's color black.

<column-break/>

¶ And Winter they assigned to the element of Water, which is cold and moist, for in that season come the great colds, the frosts, the snows, and the great rains. And because its element is Water, they made its color white.

### And they gave them this likeness according to the four humors that are created in the human body: such as Blood, which they assigned to Spring; Choller to Summer; Melancholy to Autumn; and Phlegm to Winter. Concerning the humors created in each season:

Of these four seasons that we have mentioned above, the first is Spring, and in it <term>Blood</term> <note>One of the four "humors" or bodily fluids thought to govern health and temperament in medieval medicine.</note> increases more than in all the others. ¶ And in Summer, <term>Choller</term> <note>Also known as Yellow Bile, associated with a fiery temperament.</note>. ¶ And in Autumn, <term>Melancholy</term> <note>Also known as Black Bile.</note>. ¶ And in Winter, <term>Phlegm</term>. ¶ And these seasons are distributed in this manner: ¶ Spring is temperate, for it is between Winter, which is very cold, and Summer, which is very hot; and according to the distribution of the ancient sages, it leans more toward heat than toward cold, because it partakes more of the Summer that is coming than of the trace of Winter that has passed. ¶ Summer is hot and dry, because it partakes of the heat of the Spring that passed and brings with it the heat of the Autumn to come. ¶ Autumn is temperate and leans more toward cold than toward heat, because it is between Summer, which was...

<column-break/>

very hot. And Winter is very cold; it takes more from the season that is coming than it received from the one that passed. ¶ And Winter, which comes between Autumn and Spring <note>original: *uerano*. As noted previously, in this period *verano* referred to Spring.</note>, is very cold because it takes from the nature of the Autumn that is passing and also receives from the nature of the Spring that is coming. ¶ And in this way, these four seasons take from one another. ¶ And in the likeness of these four seasons and these four humors <note>The four humors—blood, yellow bile, black bile, and phlegm—were believed in medieval medicine to regulate human health and temperament, corresponding to the four seasons and elements.</note>, they divided the game of Chess into four parts, each of its own color, according to the usage of the masters, as is appropriate for each season. Here is how this board is made for the four seasons, and how many colors its pieces <term>trebejos</term> <note>original: *trebejos*, a general term for gaming pieces or tools.</note> are, and how they are to be set up upon it.

This board must be made in this way: square, and on each side there must be eight squares <note>original: *casas*, literally 'houses'.</note>, so that there are sixty-four in total. And it must have upon it four paths in the shape of a cross <note>original: *alpa*, likely a scribal variation of *aspa*, meaning a saltire or X-shaped cross.</note>, such that one path begins at the second square from one corner of the board and goes to the second square of the opposite corner directly in a diagonal line <term>pospūta</term>. And the other path does the same. ¶ The path that goes through the white squares must be black, and the one that goes through the black squares must be white, so that they create a division between the different players' games. ¶ And these markings shown in these squares distribute which way the pawns <term>peones</term> must first move; for those that sit on the right must go to their right, and those on the left must do the same. And in the game, they shall capture diagonally <term>sosquino</term> <note>original: *sosquino*, meaning 'aslant' or 'diagonally'.</note>, just as pawns are meant to capture. ¶ And these pieces are thirty-two in total, and they must be set up at the four corners of the board; and there must be in each corner

<column-break/>

a setup of pieces consisting of one King, one Rook <term>Roque</term>, one Knight <term>Cauallo</term>, one Bishop <term>Alffil</term> <note>The *Alfil* (from Arabic *al-fil*, 'the elephant') was the medieval predecessor to the modern Bishop, moving exactly two squares diagonally and jumping over pieces.</note>, and four pawns. And all the pieces are to play with whatever value they wish according to their movement, just as they move in the other Chess which is more commonly used. ¶ And their setup is this: the Kings are placed in the furthest squares at the corners of the board. And the Rook is on one side of each King, and the Knight on the other, and the Bishop in the middle. And the pawns shall face toward one corner of the board, and the others toward the other. ¶ And in this Chess there is no Queen <note>original: *alffierza*, from the Persian *ferz* meaning 'vizier' or 'counselor'. In 1283, the piece we now call the Queen was a weaker piece that could only move one square diagonally.</note> at all, unless one is promoted from the pawns. And there are four Kings in it, and four men must play it, each with the pieces of his own color. ¶ And the colors are these four, which we have said correspond to the seasons. ¶ The pieces of Spring <note>original: *uerano*.</note> are green. ¶ Those of Summer <note>original: *Estio*.</note> are red. ¶ Those of Autumn are black <note>original: *negros*.</note>. ¶ And those of Winter are white. Concerning how they must begin to play with these pieces:

The player who has the green pieces must play first, and must begin toward the right hand, against the player who has the red pieces, just as Spring moves toward Summer. ¶ And he who has the red must play likewise against the one who has the white pieces <note>While the text says white (*blancos*), the logical seasonal rotation would be Summer attacking Autumn (Black). The text may contain a scribal error or reflect a specific variant of the rotation.</note>, guarding himself against the one who has the green so that he is not attacked. ¶ And he who has the black pieces must play toward the right hand against the one who has the dark pieces <note>original: *prietos*, a synonym for black. This passage suggests the rotation of play between the four quadrants.</note>, always guarding himself from the other player who has the red pieces, so that he is not attacked. ¶ And he who has the white pieces must do the same, guarding himself from the one with the dark pieces so that he is not attacked likewise.

# This is the Board of the tables of the four seasons of the year, which is called “The World,” which begins thus:

<note>A large, historiated initial 'P' is decorated with blue and red ink, featuring intricate white patterns and gold leaf, inhabiting the upper left portion of the first column.</note>

Since we have spoken of the board of the four seasons of the year according to how the ancient sages ordered it, it is now fitting that we show the board of the tables <note>The family of games that includes backgammon</note> which is played according to that manner. And this board is square, and the tracks <note>original: *baras*, referring to the paths or sections on the board</note> are set in a circle, and the circle is divided into four parts. In each part there are six points <note>original: *casas*, literally 'houses' or spaces</note> which are carved in the manner of a half-wheel in which the tables <note>The game pieces, similar to modern backgammon stones</note> are placed. And on this board four men are to play, each with his tables of his own color, according to the colors of the <term>Açedrex: an early form of chess derived from the Arabic *shatranj*</term> pieces that we have named. And each of these players is to have twelve tables of the colors of the aforementioned pieces, which are these: green, red, white, and black; and there are forty-eight in all. And they are played with the dice of this same chess, and they must throw a high roll <note>original: *iançar baralla*, to determine the order of play</note>: he who wins the contest plays first. And then the other who is next to him on the right hand, and so it goes all around. And he who begins first must enter his tables as the luck of the dice comes to him, just as those of

<column-break/>

sixes <note>original: *se canes*, likely referring to the highest dice outcomes</note>; and all the others likewise. And once they have entered all the tables, each one must take his own pieces to that place where the third player first entered his own—which is directly across from him—everyone playing all around to the right hand according to the luck of the dice. And he who throws a roll that he cannot use, let the next one who is right next to him on the right hand take it; and if not him, the third; and if not, let the fourth do it. And furthermore, in this game, if he throws a roll of a number that can reach the tables of the other players who are on his right hand, and finds one uncovered, he shall strike it <note>This refers to capturing an opponent's piece that is sitting alone on a point</note>. And he to whom the table belongs must return it to where he first entered it. And they must not bear off <note>Remove pieces from the board</note> any table until they have arranged them <note>original: *entabladas*</note> in the third quadrant, each one in his proper place as was said above. And the player who first bears off his own pieces shall win against the others who are behind. And this is the distribution of this game. And this is the illustration of the board, and of the tables, and of their colors in their arrangement.

# This is the Alquerque of twelve, which is played with twelve pieces.

<note>A large, illuminated decorative initial 'P' in blue with intricate red penwork flourishes and filigree patterns that extend into the left and bottom margins.</note> **S**ince we have spoken in the books above of all the manners of the games of <term>Chess|Axedrez</term>, and of <term>Dice|Dados</term>, and of <term>Tables|Tablas</term> <note>original: "tablas," a family of board games including Backgammon</note>—according to how those three wise men gave the models to King <note>original: "Zenphies"; a legendary Indian king mentioned in the prologue of this work as the patron of the three wise men</note> and how men skilled in playing categorized them.

¶ We wish now to speak here of other games that men found afterwards, which are not included in the count of those mentioned above, but which nevertheless have a part in them; such as the <term>Alquerques|ancient strategy games played on a grid, ancestors to modern Checkers</term> which share traits with Chess, Tables, and Dice. And there are some that share traits with Chess and Tables, but not with Dice.

¶ And we shall begin first with the Alquerque of twelve because it is larger than all the other Alquerques and is played with more <term>pieces|trebeios</term>. And we shall say in how many ways it is played, and with how many pieces, and for what reason it has a part of Chess, Tables, and Dice.

¶ It has a part of Chess because it is played by skill <note>original: "seso"; referring to intellect or strategic wisdom</note>, and the Alquerque is the same; and the pieces with which they are played resemble the pawns of Chess. And it is like Tables because of the <term>maneuvering|mannar</term> <note>refers to the blocking of pieces or the reaching of a stalemate/draw</note> by which they maneuver the game according to their own, and because of the points where they place the pieces. And it has a part of Dice by chance, just as dice are cast by chance on the board to see who shall have

<column-break/>

the hand <note>the first move</note>; likewise, in Alquerque they also cast lots by chance to see who moves first.

¶ And they are played in this manner: in the Alquerque there must be twenty-five places where the pieces can be placed. And the pieces are to be twenty-four; and they place the twelve of one color on one side and the other twelve of the other side in <term>rows|az</term>. And one empty place remains in the middle through which the game is commenced. And he who has the hand must place his piece from its position into that place that is empty by necessity.

¶ And through the other place: [he jumps] his piece into that same place from where the jump [was made], and he captures that [piece] which moved first, passing over it from one square to another in a straight line according to the paths of the Alquerque; and over however many pieces he passes in this way, he shall take them all. And the other player shall do the same.

¶ And always, he who had the hand must move first, arranging how he might capture some piece of those from the other side.

¶ And the other player protects himself better; for, understanding the play that the first wishes to make, he protects his piece by that same means, and does to him this same thing that the other would have done to him; and for this reason, he who has the hand must be careful.

¶ And he who poorly protects his pieces and loses the most of them is defeated.

¶ And if both players know how to play it well, they commonly end the game in a <term>draw|mannan</term>.

¶ And this is the layout of the Alquerque of twelve games, and of how they are placed in their houses <note>squares or points on the grid</note>.

BOOK OF THE <note>The header is incomplete in the source, likely intended to read "LIBRO DEL JUEGO" or "BOOK OF GAMES".</note>

<note>A rectangular miniature painting framed by a red and blue geometric border. **The game called "Hunting the Hare," which is played on the Alquerque of twelve.**

This is another game that is played on the <term>Alquerque of twelve|a 5x5 grid board with 25 points of intersection</term>: and it is called the game of **hunting the hare** <note>original: "cercar la liebre," literally "to encircle the hare"</note>. And it is played in this manner: they take one <term>piece|trebejo</term> <note>original: "trebejo," a general term for a game piece or pawn</note> and place it in the center of the Alquerque, and they place twelve of another color in a row <note>original: "en az," from the Arabic 'as-saff' meaning a rank or line of soldiers</note>, or eleven, or ten, according to the specific arrangement they agree upon for the one they are to challenge.

¶ And they play it in this way: that [single] piece must move first, and the others, however many they may be, move immediately after it.

¶ And that single piece <gloss>the hare</gloss> is cautious <note>original: "arreguardo," meaning alert or on guard</note> because they cannot capture it; rather, they must trap it in a <term>space|casa</term> <note>original: "casa," literally "house," referring to the intersections on the board grid</note> in such a way that it

<column-break/>

has no other place to go.

¶ And it [the hare] will capture as many of the others as it can by jumping over them, as we said, from one space to another in a straight line. After that single piece captures one of the others, they can no longer trap it <note>This implies that if the hunters drop below a certain number, they lose the ability to successfully encircle the hare</note>.

But if the [hunter] pieces were twelve, even after one is captured, eleven remain, and they can still trap it with them wherever they wish. They can do the same with ten if they know how to play well. But if it captures one of the ten, the nine that remain can never trap it in any way, and they lose the game because of it.

¶ And this is the distinction of this game, and this is the illustration of the Alquerque and of the games: —

# ALQUERQUE

<note>A large rectangular miniature occupies the upper half of the page, framed by a decorative border with the heraldic castles of Castile and lions of León at the corners. Against a flat blue background, two men sit on low benches facing each other. The man on the left wears a pink patterned robe, and the man on the right wears a red and grey robe. **This is the Alquerque of Nine which is played with dice; and it is played thus:**

There is another game of Alquerque that is played with eighteen <term>pieces|trebejos</term> <note>original: "trebeios," a general term for gaming pieces or pawns</note>—nine of one kind and nine of another—and they are played with dice and on these boards. This is because the marks where the pieces are placed are similar to those of <term>tables|alsan</term> <note>original: "alsa'," likely a reference to "al-sanj," a term related to the game of Tables or Backgammon</note>, and upon them are six, five, four, three, two, and ace, just as on the <term>backgammon board|tablero</term>. However, they are not distributed in their shapes as they are with the pieces. ¶ And in the playing: ¶ In the shapes, they are distributed by the marks where the boards are played, which are made in the middle of the board along with those called

<column-break/>

*iamas* <note>original: "iamas," possibly referring to the "home" or "jam" points in a tables-style game</note> or in those that are hollowed out in the manner of a half-wheel of the field. ¶ And the Alquerque is entirely composed of marks, for they must be positioned as if intertwined. ¶ And the pieces are distributed because the <term>table-men|tablas</term> <note>referring to the flat, disk-like pieces used in games like backgammon</note> are round and flattened in the manner of a nut or quarters. ¶ The others are round and tall in the manner of <term>chess pawns|peones de axedrez</term>; and these belong to the Alquerque. ¶ And in playing, they shall be distributed for this reason: ¶ He who has the lead and throws the dice first, if the points of the dice come up as six, five, and four; or more sixes; or five and three; or two aces; or both aces and a four—by whichever of these <term>throws|fuertes</term> he casts, he wins three pieces; and he shall gain an advantage once over the other player’s pieces. ¶ And if he throws in such a way that he makes a <term>ferrar|capture or alignment</term> <note>original: "ferrar," a technical term likely meaning to "lock" or "strike" a piece</note>, he shall take two pieces; and this *ferrar* is to place them just as we said they play most; and being thus equalized, he shall take that many pieces. ¶ And this same...

Between them is a square board, tilted toward the viewer, displaying the concentric square pattern of the game with dark game pieces placed on the intersections. Standing behind each player is a guard or attendant holding a long spear or staff. The miniature is positioned above two columns of text.</note>

### On how Alquerque of Nine is played

In how many ways this Alquerque of Nine is played without dice, by <term>skill|entendimiento</term> <note>original: "a entendimiento," indicating a game of logic and strategy rather than chance</note>. The players take the <term>pieces|trebejos</term> <note>original: "trebejos," a general medieval term for gaming pieces or pawns</note> in their hands and cast lots <note>original: "echan suertes"</note> for who shall place first. And he who has the lead has the advantage because, in placing the pieces, each takes his own space wherever he wishes so that he can more quickly make a <term>row of three|tercio</term> <note>original: "tercio," referring to three pieces in a line, known in modern Nine Men's Morris as a "mill"</note> while also guarding his own pieces, and take one of the other's pieces each time.

<column-break/>

...or arrange it so as to trap him, so that he has no place to move any of his pieces. ¶ And if by chance the one who has the lead fails to place his pieces well, he is defeated because another piece remains for the other player; he places it wherever he can make another row of three to match his own, as we have said, and thus wins the game. ¶ And this Alquerque they call "the one of nine" because the pieces with which it is played are nine of each color. And this is the diagram of the Alquerque and of the games, and this is its layout. —

<summary>This page describes the rules and strategic nature of "Alquerque of Nine" (Nine Men's Morris), explaining that it is a game of skill played without dice. It details the process of placing pieces to form rows of three ("mills") and the ultimate goal of trapping the opponent's pieces to win.</summary>

# ALQUERQUE

<note>A rectangular miniature with a bright blue background depicts two men playing a board game. They are seated on a tiled floor of red and green squares. Between them is a square brown game board featuring a "Nine Men's Morris" or "Alquerque de tres" layout (three concentric squares connected by midpoints). --- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous descriptions of dice and table games, transitioning into the most complex game in the collection: Astronomical Chess. This game reflects the medieval synthesis of recreational play and scientific cosmology during the reign of Alfonso X.</meta>

### This is the Board of Chess and of Tables: which is played by Astronomy.

Having explained all the three manners of games that are told in the books before this: as well the playing of chess: as of dice and tables, and having distinguished all the divisions that are in them and how they can be played, according to the most beautiful manners that men have found there through understanding and through practice.

It is appropriate now that another nature of game be shown, very noble and very rare <note>original: *estranio* — meaning unusual or remarkable</note> and very beautiful, and of great understanding for those who are knowledgeable, and especially for those who know the Art of Astronomy.

### About this game.

In this new game, it is designed according to the seven heavens in which the seven planets are. And the eighth heaven, in which the twelve Signs and the other fixed stars are. And it shows for each one how their movements proceed and how they "look" at one another by casting rays at each other—some of charity or of love, others of ill-will. And this is according to how the wise divided the sky into twelve quarters <note>these "quarters" refer to the houses of the zodiac</note>, and they show through each one of them, according to the movement of the planets, what is the look <term>aspect|the geometric relationship between planets that astrologers believe determines their influence</term> of love or of enmity.

### Concerning the Sextiles.

Among these looks <note>aspects</note> are the Sextiles, those which they call "of the third" <note>representing a 60-degree angle, or signs two spaces apart</note>, which are of love; and they look from this

<column-break/>

same Sign to the third, just as if they counted from Aries to Gemini, which is its third sign ahead, or from Aquarius, which is its third sign behind.

### Concerning the Trines.

Likewise, the Trines <note>aspects of 120 degrees</note> are the fifths, such as from Aries to Leo, which is ahead, or to Sagittarius, which is behind.

### Concerning the Squares and the Opposition.

Likewise, there are the squares <term>quadradura|A "square" aspect of 90 degrees, traditionally viewed as a relationship of tension or conflict</term> which are shown to be of ill-will, such as from Aries to Cancer or from Aries to Capricorn. And there is the opposition, which is made from this same sign to the one which is directly across from it, such as from Aries to Libra or from Cancer to Capricorn.

### Concerning the Physical Conjunction.

Besides this, there is the physical conjunction, which is the joining of two planets in a single sign; and sometimes it shows love and sometimes ill-will, and this is stronger than any other look <note>aspect</note>, whether for good or for evil, according to that which we will now set down here; and he established this game.

### Concerning the design of the Board and how it should be made.

This Board must have seven sides on the outside because seven players must play, each one with his planet. And they must have twelve quarters <note>sections</note> for whichever pieces they agree upon. And it has within it eight round circles, according to the eight heavens. And the eighth heaven <note>the outer ring</note> must be open for the figures of the twelve signs to be placed in it as they should be, which are these: Aries <note>the Ram</note>, Taurus <note>the Bull</note>, Gemini <note>the Twins</note>, Cancer <note>the Crab</note>, Leo <note>the Lion</note>, Virgo <note>the Virgin</note>, Libra <note>the Scales</note>, Scorpio <note>the Scorpion</note>, Sagittarius <note>the Archer</note>, Capricorn <note>the Goat-Horned</note>, Aquarius <note>the Water-Bearer</note>, and Pisces <note>the Fish</note>. And it is divided into twelve parts...

<summary>This page introduces "Astronomical Chess," a seven-player game designed to mirror the movements and astrological aspects of the planets and the zodiac. It explains how the game mechanics translate astrological concepts like sextiles, trines, and squares into gameplay on a heptagonal board.</summary>
<keywords>astronomical chess, astrology, zodiac signs, planetary aspects, Alfonso X, seven players, medieval science, celestial spheres</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Astronomical Chess, moving from the geometric "aspects" or "looks" of the planets to the physical construction of the board and the specific iconography of the game pieces.</meta>

...according to these twelve signs. And from the beginning of each sign, a line goes out toward the other seven circles and reaches as far as the circle of the element of fire.

The other seven circles correspond to the seven heavens in which the seven planets reside. These circles are slightly recessed, and within each of them is a square board, as we mentioned before. In these boards, there are places where the figures of the seven planets are placed when players wish to play. Each of these boards must be the color of the planet that is to be placed upon it. Furthermore, there are four round circles representing the four elements <note>The four elements—fire, air, water, and earth—were believed to sit at the center of the universe, beneath the celestial spheres.</note> situated inside these eight circles.

The first is vermilion, which represents the element of fire.
The second is a pale bluish-grey <note>original: *carçeno claro* — a color often associated with the atmosphere or flesh-tones</note>, for the element of air.
The third is white, for the element of water.
The fourth is brown, in the manner of a clod of earth and of a brown color, which represents the element of earth.

**Of the division of the seven circles**
**and how many houses they have.**

These seven aforementioned circles are divided as follows: The first is divided into eighty-four houses <note>In this context, "houses" refer to the playing spaces or landing spots on the board.</note>. The second is divided into seventy-two houses. The third is divided into sixty houses. The fourth is divided into forty-eight houses. The fifth is divided into thirty-six houses. The sixth is divided into twenty-four houses. The seventh is divided into twelve houses. And although the actual seven heavens are divided into many more parts, they could not fit here on this board in an orderly fashion; therefore, we took from that division, according to that count, the briefest version that is suitable for this game.

<column-break/>

...of the figures that the ancient wise men assigned to the seven planets and what colors they are.

**Of the figure of Saturn.**

Saturn is the first, being the highest of all. They made him in the figure of an old and thin man who walks hunched and naked, except for undergarments <note>original: *pannos menores*</note>, wrapped in a black cloth over his head. He is silent and holds his hand to his cheek like a man full of care <note>This is the classic iconographic pose of Melancholy, the temperament ruled by the planet Saturn.</note>.

**Of the figure of Jupiter.**

Jupiter, who is near him, has the appearance of a middle-aged man. He appears very cheerful, dressed in green clothes, and wears a sea-style cap <note>original: *cofia asina marina*</note> upon his head, and he holds a book before him.

**Of the figure of Mars.**

Mars has the appearance of a young man, dressed in ancient Greek armor, all of it vermilion <note>Red was the traditional color of Mars, symbolizing blood and war.</note>. In his right hand, he holds a sword drawn from its scabbard, and in the other, a man's head hanging by the hair, freshly decapitated.

**Of the figure of the Sun.**

The Sun has the appearance of a young king who wears a golden crown on his head and wears shining golden clothes. In his left hand, he holds a round orb <note>original: *maçana* — literally an "apple," used here as a royal globus cruciger representing sovereignty over the world.</note>, and in the other, a flowering branch, just like Emperors when they are crowned.

**Of the figure of Venus.**

Venus has the figure of a very beautiful young woman, with very long blonde hair over her shoulders. She has a garland of flowers on her head and is dressed in violet clothes. In her right hand, she holds a comb, and in the other, a mirror in which she gazes at herself.

**Of the figure of Mercury.**

Mercury has the appearance of a young man dressed in clothes of...

<meta>This page concludes the visual description of the planetary game pieces (Mercury and the Moon) and begins the rules for setup and movement in Astronomical Chess, a game played on a circular board that mirrors the cosmos.</meta>

...many colors, and who is writing in a book.

### Of the figure of the Moon.

The Moon has the likeness of a young woman, dressed in white cloths, and she holds with both her hands above her head a figure of a moon.

### Of how these seven Planets must be set up on the Board when they wish to begin to play with them.

In the first circle, which is closest to the circle of the Zodiac Signs, the planet they call Saturn must be placed. ¶ In the second place is the planet they call Jupiter. ¶ In the third must be the planet they call Mars. ¶ In the fourth must be the planet they call the Sun. ¶ In the fifth must be the planet they call Venus. ¶ In the sixth must be the planet they call Mercury. ¶ In the seventh must be the planet they call the Moon. ¶ And thus they are to be set in their houses <note>In medieval astrology, "houses" were the specific zodiac signs where a planet was thought to have its greatest influence or "throne."</note>. And because most of them have designated houses in their Zodiac Signs, we place each one in that which is most appropriate, as follows: ¶ Saturn in Aquarius. ¶ And Jupiter in Sagittarius. ¶ And Mars in Scorpio. ¶ And the Sun in Leo. ¶ And Venus in Taurus. ¶ And Mercury in Virgo. ¶ And the Moon in Cancer <note>original: *Canro*</note>.

### Of how this game of chess is to be played with one die and no more.

This game is said to be played in this manner: that each of the players casts their lot with a die that has seven faces, and has upon it dots from one to seven, so that each one knows with which pla-

<column-break/>

-net they must play: ¶ And he who receives by luck the seven, which are the most dots, shall have Saturn. ¶ And he who receives the six, shall have Jupiter. ¶ And he who receives the five, shall have Mars. ¶ And he who receives the four, shall have the Sun. ¶ And he who receives the three dots, shall have Venus. ¶ And he who receives the two, shall have Mercury. ¶ And he who receives the one, shall have the Moon. ¶ And furthermore, they cast the *taya* <note>original: *taya* — a preliminary toss to determine who goes first, often involving a wager</note>, and he who wins it shall have the lead; he casts the die, and however many dots it shows him, that is how many houses he must move with his planet. And if he does not move that many houses because he would leave the Zodiac Sign in which he resides, he neither wins nor loses; but if he should enter into another Sign, he loses or wins according to the aspect <note>The "aspect" refers to the geometric relationship and angular distance between planets, which determines their astrological influence on one another.</note> he has with the other planets. ¶ And if this planet enters the sign of another: he wins from him ten and twelve <note>These numbers likely refer to the stakes or betting units used in the game.</note>. If it enters into its course: he wins three of twelve. And if it enters into its quadrature <term>quadrature: an astrological "aspect" where two planets are 90 degrees apart, usually considered a position of conflict</term>: he loses three of twelve. And if it enters into its opposition <term>opposition: an aspect where two planets are 180 degrees apart, or directly across the board from each other</term>: he loses six of twelve. And if he enters into its conjunction <term>conjunction: when two planets occupy the same space or "house"</term>: he must lose twelve. ¶ And in this way this game is to be played. ¶ And this is the figure of the board, and of the seven planets, and how they are set upon it.

<summary>This page describes the iconography of the Moon piece and outlines the rules for Astronomical Chess, including the use of a unique seven-sided die and scoring based on astrological aspects.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the "Book of Tables," specifically detailing a unique astronomical variant of a backgammon-style game. The previous pages likely described the seven-sided dice used in this game.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE TABLES

### **This is the Board of the tables according to the nature of the Signs, which is played by Astronomy.**

**This** Board of Tables is to be made of wood, divided in half just like the chessboard, both on the inside and the outside. On the side that is on the inside, there are to be seven houses <note>These are the triangular "points" or spaces on a backgammon-style board.</note>. And this should be on each of the other sides. Between one side and the other, there must be a partition that divides both sides. And that partition is to have a long line that reaches the middle point of the board. And the tables <term>tables|flat, disc-shaped game pieces, similar to modern backgammon stones</term> on these sides are to be the color of the planets. There are to be as many tables as there are houses. And above each side, the planet to which it belongs is to be depicted in its painting, and colored in the color that suits it: **Saturn** in black, **Jupiter** in green, **Mars** in red <note>original: "vermecio," referring to vermilion</note>, **The Sun** in yellow, **Venus** in violet, **Mercury** in many diverse colors, and **The Moon** in white. And therefore, these tables that belong to those planets must be of their respective color. And the setup <note>original: "entablamiento"</note> is to be in this manner: all seven tables are placed in the first house of the series that is on the left hand, and they always move in play toward the right hand, according to the points shown

<column-break/>

by the dice of the Seven ways <note>These are special seven-sided dice designed to correspond to the seven planets.</note> that we mentioned above. And one cannot "hit" <note>The text uses "mienta," likely meaning a player cannot land on or capture a point</note> the house in which the pieces are stacked, nor the other house to the right where those seven tables are set up, unless only one table remains there: <unclear>because</unclear> then the empty house may take it as it enters, according to how the dice are played. And the play is in this manner: each of the players shall have seven stakes <term>tantos|chips or markers used for scoring or betting</term> of whatever value of money they agree upon, or of whatever currency they wish. And if one takes another's table, he is not to remove it from the board; instead, he should take a coin <note>original: "un canto"</note> for it. And for as many as he takes, they all go around in a circle until the whole game is finished by one of those who are playing; that person remains the victor. And this is the illustration of the Board and of the tables.

> This Book was begun and finished in the city of **Seville**, by command of the very noble **King Don Alfonso**, son of the very noble **King Don Ferdinand** and of **Queen Lady Beatrice**, **Lord** of **Castile**, **Leon**, **Toledo**, **Galicia**, **Seville**, **Cordoba**, **Murcia**, **Jaén**, **Badajoz**, and the **Algarve**, in the thirty-second year that the aforementioned **King** reigned. In the Era of one thousand three hundred and twenty-one years. <note>The "Spanish Era" calendar was 38 years ahead of the AD system; the year 1321 refers to 1283 AD.</note>
